LinkedIn doesn't publish a price for Recruiter or Recruiter Professional Services. Its Recruiter page says pricing "varies based on your organization's needs," and its plan comparison lists features, not prices. The figures below are reported by third-party pricing trackers and agencies that have seen quotes, so treat them as ranges and ask LinkedIn for a written quote. Pin's own LinkedIn Recruiter pricing breakdown covers each plan in more detail.

InMail and network details are from LinkedIn's plan comparison (October 2026). Prices are tracker-reported (July to September 2026), and annual Lite totals range from $1,680 to $2,040.

It depends on how much of your hiring runs through LinkedIn. One seat at the reported range costs about 1.8 to 2.4 times SHRM's 2025 benchmark of $5,475 average cost per hire for a nonexecutive role, so a seat has to contribute hires, not just searches. Teams weighing the lower-priced Lite plan can see where its limits start in Pin's guide to Recruiter Lite vs full Recruiter.

LinkedIn's January 2026 research found that two-thirds (66%) of recruiters say it has become harder to find qualified talent, and 93% plan to increase their use of AI in 2026.

A seat usually earns its cost when most of your candidates are on LinkedIn, you use the InMail allotment and your reply rates stay healthy.

LinkedIn is not standing still: Recruiter now builds search filters from a plain-language description, drafts AI-assisted messages and offers the Hiring Assistant add-on. The limits below are about design, checked against LinkedIn's pages in October 2026.

We checked each vendor's public pricing and product pages in October 2026. Pin is covered first because it spans the widest scope; the others follow in no ranked order.

Pin (pin.com) is an AI recruiting platform that handles candidate sourcing, outreach and interview scheduling in one product. For teams replacing LinkedIn Recruiter, Pin is the most direct one-tool replacement.

Best for: teams and agencies replacing LinkedIn Recruiter that want broader search, outreach and scheduling in one seat-priced tool with published prices. See also AI recruiting tools for agencies.

SeekOut is a sourcing platform for finding and engaging candidates across 1B+ profiles and your ATS. Its pricing page lists Sourcing Core at $179 a month, or $149 billed annually, with three seats, AI multi-step campaigns and a 14-day free trial; higher tiers are custom. The page lists email outreach and no interview scheduling.

Good for: small sourcing teams that want deep search, though outreach is email only, scheduling isn't listed and higher tiers are custom.

hireEZ is a talent acquisition platform that adds sourcing, CRM, outreach, screening and scheduling on top of your ATS. It lists 1B+ profiles across 45+ platforms, email, SMS and InMail engagement, and an AI Scheduler. Team pricing isn't published, and solo recruiters start at $494 a month.

Good for: mid-size and enterprise teams consolidating tools around an existing ATS, though team pricing is unpublished and solo starts at $494 a month.

Gem is a recruiting platform that layers a CRM, AI sourcing, outreach, scheduling and analytics on top of your ATS. It searches 800M+ profiles and runs multi-channel sequences, though its pricing page doesn't name the channels. Scheduling is an add-on to Gem + your ATS. Pricing is custom by headcount; the only published number is $130 a month for startups with 1 to 10 employees on yearly billing.

Good for: growth-stage and larger teams that want CRM and scheduling in one stack, though pricing is sales-led and scheduling is an add-on.

Findem is an AI talent-intelligence platform. Its site lists Multichannel Sourcing, a Talent CRM and a Scheduling Agent, and says it maps 1B+ career paths. Pricing isn't published; you request a demo.

Good for: larger talent teams that want attribute-rich search, though buying is demo-only and no price is published.

Fetcher is a sourcing platform that pairs a 500M+ candidate database with optional done-for-you sourcing. Its pricing page lists a custom Enterprise tier, Amplify at $649 a month on annual billing ($849 monthly), Growth at $379 ($499 monthly) and Self-serve at $115. It lists email outreach and a LinkedIn sourcing plugin, and no scheduling.

Good for: small teams that want extra sourcing capacity without building a process first, though scheduling isn't listed and the $115 entry tier jumps to $379.

"Not listed" means absent from the vendor's pricing page, not necessarily from the product.

#10876. AI Agents Need Idempotency: The Distributed Systems Problem Hiding Inside Agentic AI

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A customer wants a $480 refund for a faulty part of a shipment he received. After waiting for 30 seconds for the payment API to process the refund, the customer support AI sends another request. It ultimately is fulfilled. However, as it turns out, the first request had also been fulfilled. The only thing missing was the notification of the refund for the customer. So the customer received two refunds of $480 each instead of the one he was asking for.

The AI was acting appropriately within its given constraints; the surrounding system couldn’t tell if the initial action had already been taken and failed to deliver the result. A well-known problem in distributed systems is gaining more notoriety as AI-powered agents increasingly perform actions beyond simply generating text. Idempotence is the solution to this problem.

Idempotence means an action can be performed multiple times with the same result as performing it once. This concept is particularly important for an AI agent that issues refunds, creates tickets, sends emails, or updates business applications.

AI agents as distributed software components have simple processes.

Think → choose a tool → call an API → receive a result → decide what to do next.

As we have outlined, many of the agent actions will utilize services and networks that can have issues from time to time. Consider the simple action of asking a payment provider to issue a refund. There are three possible outcomes:

The payment service never gets the request.

The refund went through, and the agent got the answer.

The refund went through, but the agent never received it back.

The most severe case, however, is when the time to complete an operation exceeds the timeout, but the operation does complete successfully. Since there is no answer, the software agent never learns of the operation's successful completion and must attempt to complete the operation again.

Distributed systems have dealt with the issues of unreliable networks for a long time already (retries, duplicate requests, etc.). By introducing agents, we give the software more freedom to choose when and how to proceed.

Consider an agent with these tools: search_customer(), check_inventory(), refund_payment(), send_email(), create_order(), delete_account()

Calling search_customer() twice will usually not cause much trouble.

In other words, an agent runtime should be able to tell if a tool only gets data or makes changes to the outside world. In the second group are things like sending money, getting money back, placing orders, writing messages, making accounts, and changing infrastructure.

There are side effects to these treatments. It's risky to think that agents can just retry every failed tool call as they get access to more business systems.

One common way to handle the duplicate requests issue is to implement idempotency keys.

Idempotency-Key: refund-order-123-run-842

The service stores the idempotency key and the result of processing the associated request.

It allows the refund to be processed the first time the idempotency key is sent to the service by the agent. Thereafter, if the response to the refund is lost and the agent attempts to resubmit the refund with the same idempotency key, the service will recognize that the refund has already been processed and return the previous result to the agent instead of processing another refund.

Many of the world’s leading payment companies, including Stripe, PayPal, and Square, use idempotency keys to allow their customers to safely retry requests.

However, one important rule for agents is that the agent software or workflow system—not the AI model—creates the idempotency key, and it is up to date.

By the time the model retries the same step, the Operation ID will have remained the same. This means the system can recognize the request as another attempt to execute the same action as before and not a new action.

Things could be easy if we could tell the model to ‘remember if it has already issued one of these before issuing another.' But even in the production environment, this approach is not sufficient. The agent could crash. The context could go away. The workflow could be taken off to be executed on another machine. The problem is that the model lacks sufficient information about what has or has not happened in external systems. Instead, the system should keep a record of the execution state outside of the model. The model determines what should happen. The runtime determines safe execution.

Modern agent tools include a name, description, and input parameters for the production system. More importantly, the agent runtime also needs to know about the behavior of the individual tool. For example, ‘refund_order’ modifies the application outside this tool, so the operation must be re-executed with the same actionID used for the initial failure. Also, as an example, refunds larger than $5,000 require manual approval. On the other hand, a ‘search_customer’ action is only a read, so re-execution can occur without human intervention. Based on this information, the OS makes decisions about the danger of the individual tool call. Automatic re-execution is still possible, even if the search fails. However, refunds require a bit more caution to avoid double posting of money. Thus, in addition to the input models for the AI tools used in real life, so-called “execution rules” are also required. These rules state when re-execution, increased security, or human intervention is required.

Suppose an AI agent were to perform the following three actions: 1) Make whole the customer, 2) Send a confirmation email with the order update and a proof, and 3) Check the status of the order in process. We had described how stopping the AI from paying out twice was only half the problem; the system also needed to know where the work had stopped. To that end, we’d like to introduce the concept of durable execution. For durable execution, the agent must do more than simply remember the actions it has taken. We need to keep track of the progress of the actions of the AI. The refund action was marked complete, but the email action was not sent yet. Thus, when the agent is restarted, it continues from where it left off rather than starting over. To create reliable AI agents, we need a few things: stable operation IDs, irrevocable actions, durable state, controlled retries, and logging. There are also cases where one wants to perform a recovery or compensating action (e.g., the agent makes a reservation but then fails to perform the subsequent action). The recovery action would be to cancel the reservation that had been made.

While controls with a person in the loop are mostly suitable for fine-grained actions that cannot be undone, idempotence ensures that an action is not accidentally executed more than once, and human approval is required to decide whether the action should be executed in the first place. This point is illustrated by an example: An employee correctly approves a $10,000 refund. However, the payment request somehow times out and is automatically re-executed several times. Without adequate idempotency security, the customer might end up with even more refunds than intended. Note, however, that a person’s approval cannot prevent double killing in such high-risk agent processes. Therefore, additional decision controls and corresponding execution controls are usually required to ensure that all actions are executed safely and correctly.

There are 5 simple questions that need to be answered before allowing an AI agent to take real-world actions.

Improving the model alone will not make the agent reliable unless we can answer all of these questions.

Much work in AI today focuses on making agents smarter, better at planning, and better to use as tools. But running agents in production has its own problems. API calls time out, processes crash, networks fail, and responses get lost, and the smartest agent can try again and again until it works. These are not new problems. Engineers working on distributed systems have been struggling with these same issues for decades. So long as the AI agent is confined to a simple test environment, of course it can query payment systems, customer information, and corporate tools. But when it is turned loose on the production corporation’s infrastructure (with all the payment systems, customer information, and corporate tools that entails), it must also be reliable. A smart model can figure out that a customer is due a refund. But a strong system can only give that customer a refund once. So the important question to ask of an AI agent before turning loose on it is, "Some powerful new tool: what happens if the action worked?" The answer will tell you whether or not the AI agent is currently able to operate reliably in production.

#10871. Web-Ready AI Images: Formats, Sizes, and srcset Done Right

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI image generation has become a normal part of many web projects. However, the image a model produces is rarely the image a browser should receive. A single large PNG straight from a generator can weigh several megabytes, ignore the visitor's screen size, and slow down your Largest Contentful Paint (LCP) before the page has a chance to impress anyone.

The good news is that the fix is not complicated. If you treat the generated image as a high-quality master file and run it through a small, repeatable pipeline, you can ship images that look sharp on every device and load quickly on slow connections.

This tutorial walks through that pipeline from start to finish: generating at the right size and aspect ratio, choosing modern formats, creating responsive variants with Node.js, writing srcset and sizes correctly, and avoiding the performance mistakes that show up most often in audits.

Image generators are built to produce visually impressive results, not optimized web assets. Their output is usually a single file at one resolution, often in PNG format, and sometimes with an aspect ratio that does not match your layout.

Sending that file directly to the browser creates three problems. First, mobile visitors download far more pixels than their screens can display. Second, PNG is a poor choice for photographic images because its lossless compression produces large files. Third, without width and height information, the browser cannot reserve space for the image, which causes layout shifts as the page loads.

A web pipeline solves all three. You generate one master image, export multiple sizes in modern formats, and let the browser choose the best version for each visitor.

Optimization starts before you export anything. The most efficient pipeline begins with an image that already fits your layout.

Look at where the image will appear and note its aspect ratio at each breakpoint. A full-width hero might use 16:9 on desktop, while a blog thumbnail might use 4:3 or 1:1. Then identify the largest size the image will ever display. For a full-width hero, that could be 1920 pixels wide on large screens, or more if you support high-density displays at wide viewports.

Your master image should be at least as large as that maximum rendered width. Upscaling a small image later almost always introduces blurriness or artifacts.

Modern image-generation models can follow detailed instructions, so describe the technical requirements in your prompt, not just the subject. Specify the aspect ratio, intended display size, visual style, and any areas that need to remain clear for HTML text. The exact controls available will depend on the tool you use, so check its official documentation before building a workflow around a particular feature.

A practical web-focused prompt might read: "hero image of a modern home office at sunrise, soft natural light, calm blue and warm wood tones, clear empty space on the left third for headline text, no text or logos in the image."

That last instruction matters. Avoid baking headlines or buttons into images. Text inside an image cannot be translated, resized for accessibility, or indexed reliably, and it often becomes unreadable at small sizes. Keep text in HTML and use the image for visuals only.

Once you have a master file, decide which formats to serve. For most photographic and illustrative AI images, a combination of AVIF, WebP, and JPEG covers every modern browser while keeping file sizes small.

AVIF typically offers the strongest compression for photographic content and is supported by all major modern browsers, including Safari 16 and later. WebP is slightly less efficient but encodes faster and has near-universal support. JPEG remains a reliable fallback for older browsers and email clients.

Use PNG only when you specifically need lossless quality, such as for sharp-edged graphics or screenshots. Both AVIF and WebP support transparency, so you rarely need PNG for images with transparent backgrounds anymore.

Creating a dozen versions of every image by hand is tedious and error-prone. Instead, script the process. The Sharp documentation explains how to resize images and export them in different formats.

Then create a script that exports several widths in each format:

const widths = [480, 768, 1080, 1440, 1920];

{ ext: 'avif', format: 'avif', options: { quality: 50 } },

{ ext: 'webp', format: 'webp', options: { quality: 75 } },

{ ext: 'jpg', format: 'jpeg', options: { quality: 80, mozjpeg: true } },

for (const { ext, format, options } of formats) {

.resize({ width, withoutEnlargement: true })

Save the file as an ES module, such as build-images.mjs, and run it with Node. The withoutEnlargement option prevents sharp from upscaling the master when a target width is larger than the original.

The quality values above are reasonable starting points, not universal rules. AVIF and WebP quality scales do not map directly to JPEG quality, so compare outputs visually at the sizes your users will actually see. Smooth gradients, which are common in AI-generated skies and backgrounds, are especially prone to banding at aggressive settings.

The MDN responsive images guide explains how srcset and sizes work. The srcset attribute lists available image files and their intrinsic widths. The sizes attribute tells the browser how wide the image will be displayed in the layout. With both pieces of information, the browser can choose the smallest file that still looks sharp on the visitor's screen, including high-density displays.

Here is an example for an image inside an article column that is 720 pixels wide on larger screens and nearly full width on smaller ones:

alt="A modern home office at sunrise with a wooden desk and soft natural light"

The most common mistake is omitting sizes or leaving it at 100vw for images that are not full width. When that happens, the browser assumes the image fills the entire viewport and downloads a much larger file than necessary. Always match sizes to your actual CSS layout.

To offer AVIF and WebP with a JPEG fallback, wrap your image in a picture element. The browser checks each source in order and uses the first format it supports. See the MDN documentation for the picture element for the full syntax.

srcset="dist/hero-768.avif 768w, dist/hero-1440.avif 1440w, dist/hero-1920.avif 1920w"

srcset="dist/hero-768.webp 768w, dist/hero-1440.webp 1440w, dist/hero-1920.webp 1920w"

srcset="dist/hero-768.jpg 768w, dist/hero-1440.jpg 1440w, dist/hero-1920.jpg 1920w"

alt="A modern home office at sunrise with a wooden desk and soft natural light"

The img element is still required. It holds the fallback file, the dimensions, the alt text, and the loading attributes, while the source elements simply provide alternative formats.

If you need a different crop on mobile, such as a square version of a wide hero, add media attributes to your source elements. This technique is called art direction, and it is a good reason to generate or crop a separate master image for small screens rather than relying on CSS to hide important parts of a wide composition.

Always include width and height attributes on your img element. Modern browsers use them to calculate the image's aspect ratio and reserve the correct space before the file downloads, which helps prevent Cumulative Layout Shift. In CSS, set the image to width: 100% and height: auto so it scales responsively while keeping its proportions.

Use loading="lazy" for images below the fold, but never on your hero or other LCP image. Lazy loading your most important image delays it unnecessarily. Instead, add fetchpriority="high" to the LCP image so the browser downloads it early.

Write alt text that describes the image's purpose in context. If an image is purely decorative, use an empty alt attribute so screen readers skip it. Generated images do not come with meaningful descriptions, so this step always requires human judgment.

Use descriptive file names, such as home-office-sunrise-1440.avif, instead of generic names like image-final-v3.png. Clear names make assets easier to manage and give search engines a small amount of extra context.

Be aware of metadata. Some AI image providers, including OpenAI, embed C2PA provenance data that identifies an image as AI-generated. sharp strips most metadata by default, which reduces file size but also removes that provenance information. If transparency matters to your project or your client, recent versions of sharp offer a keepMetadata() method, and you can also disclose AI-generated imagery in captions or credits.

In Chrome DevTools, open the Network panel, filter by images, and resize the viewport or switch to device emulation. Reload the page and confirm that smaller files load on smaller screens. You can also select the image in the Elements panel and check its currentSrc property in the console to see which file the browser actually chose.

Then run Lighthouse or PageSpeed Insights. Look for warnings about properly sized images, modern image formats, and LCP.

Not always. AVIF often produces smaller files for photographic content, but it encodes more slowly. Serving both with a JPEG fallback is a safe default.

Four to six widths usually cover most layouts. Choose them based on your breakpoints and the largest rendered size of each image.

Review the current terms of service for the model and the platform you use. Licensing terms can differ between providers and plans.

AI image generation removes much of the cost and time of creating custom visuals, but it does not remove the need for good web engineering. Generate a high-quality master at the right aspect ratio, export responsive variants in modern formats, write accurate srcset and sizes values, protect your LCP and layout stability, and test everything before you ship.

Once the pipeline is scripted, every new image takes seconds to optimize. That combination gives you the creative speed of AI and the performance your visitors expect.

#10864. Best Claude Tracking Tools in 2026: 6 Options Compared

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most teams set up AI visibility tracking for ChatGPT, add Google, and stop there. Claude tends to come up later, usually when someone notices that half the engineers, analysts and founders they know use it every day. That instinct is sound. Claude's audience leans towards the people who research software and services before they buy.

The numbers back it up. Similarweb put Claude at 3.3% of AI chatbot web visits in February 2026 and 9.2% in May 2026, close to three times the share in three months, while ChatGPT's share kept falling. Those are web visits only. Usage in the Claude apps, in Claude Code and through the API does not show up in that figure, so 9.2% is a floor.

This guide compares six tools that track Claude. Peec AI comes first because it connects Claude answers to the pages behind them. The other five cover different budgets and needs.

A Claude tracking tool sends a fixed set of prompts to Claude on a schedule and records what comes back: whether your brand is named, where in the answer, how it is described, and which sources the answer relied on. Run daily, those records become trends you can compare against competitors.

It is not a rank tracker. Claude gives no list of ten blue links, so the useful numbers are mention rate, position inside the answer, sentiment and cited sources.

How the tool queries Claude. Some tools send your prompts to Anthropic's API. Others drive the Claude app. The two can give different answers, because the app has its own web search behaviour and the API, queried cold, often does not. Neither is wrong. Your buyers use the app, and the API is cheaper to run at scale. What matters is that you know which one you are looking at and that it stays the same over time.

Which plan includes Claude. Claude is a premium engine for several vendors. It can sit on the entry plan, on a higher tier or on Enterprise only, and a free tier often leaves it out.

What it costs per run. Credit-based tools can charge more for Claude than for other engines. Check the credit formula before you size a plan.

Source data. A mention rate says whether Claude names you. Source data says why it does not. Pick a tool that shows the pages Claude read.

Prompt volume. 50 to 150 prompts per brand is a realistic start. Fewer than 15 is mostly noise. Check how many prompts the cheaper tiers cover with Claude specifically: on credit-based tools Claude costs more per run, so the entry plan can cover far fewer Claude prompts than the headline number suggests.

Peec AI is a Berlin-based AI search analytics platform that tracks visibility, position, sentiment and share of voice across AI engines, then shows which sources drive those answers. Claude is part of the Enterprise plan and the agency Scale plan, tracked next to ChatGPT, Gemini, Perplexity, Microsoft Copilot and both Google surfaces. That makes it the pick for teams that want Claude as one engine in a wider view rather than as a stand-alone report.

Best for: Teams that track Claude together with ChatGPT and Google and want to know which pages to work on, not only where they stand.

Scrunch AI tracks Claude on its Enterprise plan, alongside nine engines in total. The published Core plan at $250 a month covers four engines and leaves Claude out, so it suits teams that start with ChatGPT, Perplexity, Google and Copilot and add Claude when they move to a quote.

Best for: Teams that want a published entry price now and add Claude through an Enterprise contract later.

Rankscale puts Claude on every paid plan from $99 a month and covers the widest engine list at that price: Claude, Copilot, ChatGPT, Perplexity, Gemini, both Google surfaces, DeepSeek and Mistral. It is credit-based, and the formula is published, which matters because Claude costs 2 credits per run, more than most engines. On Pro, 1,200 credits cover about 20 prompts run daily on Claude alone.

Best for: Small teams and agencies that want Claude and a long engine list for the least money.

AthenaHQ was built by two engineers who worked on Google Search and DeepMind. Claude is included from the Starter plan, among eleven engines. The free Essential tier does not include Claude, so it shows you the other engines first. Its optimisation agent drafts on-page and off-page recommendations, which suits teams that have the analysis but not the hands to act on it.

Best for: Teams short on people to write the follow-up work.

Profound is the shortest path for companies where an information security review decides the purchase. It lists SOC 2, SSO and SAML on Enterprise and raised a $96M Series C at a $1B valuation in February 2026. Claude is part of the Enterprise engine list, which runs to up to nine engines.

Best for: Enterprises that need a compliance posture on paper before they can buy.

Nightwatch is worth a look if you want Claude mentions next to the keyword rankings you already track and you are not paying for one of the large SEO suites. AI tracking is included in the plan price.

Best for: SEO teams that want one dashboard for classic rankings and Claude.

If Claude is one engine among several and you need to know what to fix, choose the tool with source analysis. If Claude is the only new engine and budget decides, Rankscale and Nightwatch cost the least. If you have a security review to pass, start with Profound or Peec AI's Enterprise plan. If you have no Claude coverage at all and cannot add a vendor, a few tools let you connect your own Anthropic API key, and Anthropic then bills you directly for the runs.

Claude is now a real share of the AI answers your buyers read, and its answers often differ from ChatGPT's. A single-engine number gives you false confidence in either direction. Peec AI is the strongest choice for teams that want Claude as part of a full view of sources, gaps and actions, with the caveat that Claude requires the Enterprise plan or, for agencies, the Scale plan. For the cheapest route to Claude coverage, Rankscale and Nightwatch are the practical options, though Rankscale's entry plan covers only about 20 daily Claude prompts, against 50 AI prompts on Nightwatch's entry plan. Whichever you choose, ask how the tool queries Claude and keep that method fixed. A tool measures. What moves the number is being on the pages Claude reads, and the best Claude tracking tools show you which ones those are.

No. If you can add one engine after ChatGPT, add Gemini, because its share is about three times larger. Add Claude third, and earlier if your buyers are technical.

Does Claude cite the same sources as ChatGPT?

Often not, and that is the practical reason to track it. Being named in one engine tells you little about the other.

With some tools, yes. Gauge lets you connect your own key on any plan, and Anthropic bills you directly for the runs. It is a cheap way to add Claude if your main tool does not include it.

How do I turn Claude tracking results into something I can act on?

Look for source data, not only a mention rate. A mention rate says whether Claude names you, while source data shows which pages Claude relied on and where competitors are cited and you are not. Peec AI classifies every cited page, reports the gaps and ranks recommended actions from them, and AthenaHQ goes a step further by drafting follow-up content. Tools that stop at mention counts tell you where you stand but not what to change.

#10858. 8 Checks Developers Should Add Before Importing PDF Tables into an Application

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A PDF table can look correct and still produce unreliable application data. A wrapped description becomes two rows. An account code loses its leading zeros. A repeated page header is imported as a transaction. A missing decimal separator changes an amount without triggering an obvious error.

These problems are easy to overlook when an exported spreadsheet opens successfully. But opening a workbook proves very little about whether its contents belong in a database.

For developers building reporting dashboards, inventory systems, or document-import features, PDF extraction should feed a validation workflow. The following eight checks help separate readable output from data your application can safely process.

The examples use fictional invoice-style records and Python. They demonstrate a narrow validation contract rather than a complete PDF conversion service.

Not all PDFs contain the same kind of information.

A digitally generated document may include selectable text and vector lines. A scanned document may contain only page images. A mixed document can contain both, sometimes with an existing OCR layer that is incomplete or inaccurate.

Inspect representative pages before committing to an extraction strategy. Check whether text is selectable, whether copied Check parsing and validation behavior against fixtures

The pdfplumber documentation explains its text and table capabilities, including visual debugging. Its strengths are primarily associated with machine-generated PDFs; it is not an OCR engine.

OCRmyPDF adds a searchable text layer to scans. That does not, by itself, reconstruct a correct table schema. You still need to determine which text belongs in each row and column.

Practical check: Build a sample set containing a clean digital PDF, a scan, and a multi-page table before evaluating the extraction path.

A conversion step should create an inspectable intermediate result. It should not automatically authorize database writes.

For workflows that need a spreadsheet as the intermediate format, Lido is one option for converting PDF content to Excel. Evaluate the output against representative documents, then apply the same schema and reconciliation checks you would use with a custom extraction script.

Commit accepted records to the application.

This structure lets you replace an extraction tool without changing every downstream rule. It also provides a place to inspect failures before they become production records.

SitePoint’s guide to parsing spreadsheet data with Python provides background on working with spreadsheet formats in an application workflow.

Practical check: The importer should be able to reject a workbook even when conversion completed successfully.

Column names alone are not a sufficient contract. A field called “Date” could represent an invoice date, payment date, or service date.

Decide how repeated headers, subtotal rows, footnotes, and blank lines will be identified. Do not let them disappear through undocumented cleanup rules.

Load a workbook with deliberate type handling

The pandas read_excel reference documents these loading options. Using strings limits unwanted interpretation at this stage, while keep_default_na=False preserves values that would otherwise match default missing-value markers.

However, reading a cell as text cannot recover information already lost upstream. If a converter stored 00127 as the number 127, this setting will not reconstruct the original ID.

Practical check: Compare identifiers against the original document, including examples with leading zeros.

Removing every comma and currency symbol may look convenient, but it hides ambiguity. The strings follow different conventions.

Choose an explicit format per document family. The following parser accepts a deliberately narrow format: ASCII digits, optional correctly grouped thousands commas, a decimal point, and up to two decimal places. It permits a leading minus sign for adjustments.

This intentionally rejects parentheses, currency symbols, exponent notation, and comma-decimal formatting. Those formats may be valid in other documents, but supporting them requires an explicit rule.

Python’s Decimal documentation explains decimal arithmetic. Constructing a Decimal directly from the validated string avoids first introducing a binary floating-point representation.

Practical check: Keep the original extracted value alongside the normalized value, and reconcile the result against other evidence.

A hundred extracted rows are not necessarily the correct hundred rows. One line may be missing while another is duplicated.

Required fields are present and nonempty.

Dates are valid under the declared format.

Line identifiers are unique within the correct scope.

Recognized line-item totals reconcile with the appropriate subtotal.

Each accepted row has a traceable source location.

For a document whose stated subtotal is exactly the sum of its line amounts:

Do not compare that subtotal directly with a grand total containing tax, shipping, or other adjustments. First model those components.

Avoid a generic tolerance such as “accept differences below one dollar.” If the document requires rounding, define where rounding occurs, the currency precision, and the permitted discrepancy.

SitePoint’s overview of data preprocessing provides additional context for separating cleaning, transformation, and integration.

Practical check: Inject one missing row and one duplicate into your fixtures. Confirm that the workflow detects both.

When a record fails validation, a reviewer should not have to search a long PDF without context.

Keep a reference to the source document, page number, extraction run, and original field value. Where the extractor exposes cell coordinates, retain them so a review interface can highlight the relevant region.

Distinguish “not extracted,” “blank in source,” and “not applicable.” Turning all three into an empty string makes it harder to understand what happened.

Confidence scores can help prioritize work, but they are not interchangeable across tools and do not replace business validation. A highly confident extraction can still be placed in the wrong column.

Practical check: Ask someone unfamiliar with the document to resolve a flagged record using only the review interface and its source reference.

An import may stop after extraction, during validation, or while writing records. Retrying should not create another copy of every accepted line.

Assign each import a stable identity. A document hash can help identify byte-identical uploads, while an explicit tenant or account scope prevents unrelated users’ imports from being treated as one.

A hash alone is insufficient for every duplicate policy. Two differently encoded PDFs may represent the same invoice, and one file may legitimately need reprocessing after a parser correction.

Track both the document identity and processing version. Separate extraction attempts from committed business records.

At the database boundary, define the appropriate uniqueness rule and use a transaction for the unit you promise to import atomically. For example, invoice lines might be unique by account, invoice identity, and line number—not by invoice ID alone across all customers.

When reprocessing changes existing values, make that an explicit replacement or correction workflow rather than an unnoticed side effect of retrying.

Practical check: Run the same accepted import twice and verify that the application record count remains unchanged.

The most valuable fixtures are not always the most visually complicated. A tiny change can alter business meaning while producing valid-looking data.

Use pytest parameterization to exercise numeric-format boundarie. SitePoint’s Python unit-testing guide explains the broader testing workflow.

Parser tests do not verify the extraction engine. Add end-to-end fixtures with manually verified expected records for repeated headers, wrapped descriptions, scans, and multi-page tables.

Measure field accuracy and the number of documents that pass all required checks. A strong average can conceal a weak result on the one field your application depends on most.

Practical check: Retain a regression fixture for every meaningful production failure, using synthetic or appropriately sanitized documents.

PDF conversion produces candidate data. Your application decides whether that data is complete, consistent, and suitable for use.

A dependable workflow identifies the source format, preserves extraction evidence, enforces explicit column rules, reconciles values, and makes retries predictable.

Start with one document family and a small verified fixture set. Expand only when you can explain both why a record was accepted and how a reviewer can investigate one that was rejected.

#10853. Beyond the Password Reset: Building Recovery Experiences That Protect Online Shopping

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An unfamiliar delivery address appears in a shopper’s account. Minutes later, an order confirmation arrives for something they never purchased. They try to sign in, but their password no longer works.

For the consumer, this is an urgent problem: stop the misuse, recover access, and understand what happened. For developers, it exposes a gap between building a login screen and building a service that remains useful when that login is compromised.

This article examines recovery from a business-to-consumer perspective: what an individual shopper needs, and how developers can translate those needs into safer interfaces and backend controls.

Account takeover and identity theft can overlap, but they are different problems.

Account takeover means someone has gained unauthorized control of an existing account. Identity theft involves misuse of someone’s personal information. Recovering access to one shopping account does not necessarily resolve misuse elsewhere.

Design the interface around questions a worried customer can answer:

A recovery page should explain the scope of help. “Recover your shopping account” is a specific promise. “Restore your identity” describes a much broader outcome that an ordinary retailer cannot deliver on its own.

Before building the workflow, list the consumer-visible assets involved: account access, addresses, order history, payment references, contact details, and support messages. Each needs its own review or remediation step.

A shopper who cannot sign in must still be able to locate help. Provide a public “My account may be compromised” entry point alongside ordinary password recovery.

Separate the two journeys. Someone who forgot a password usually needs a credential reset. Someone reporting suspicious activity may also need unauthorized changes reversed, orders investigated, and existing access revoked.

Avoid using a submitted email address as proof of ownership. A public report can initiate triage, but it should not expose order history or authorize sensitive changes.

OWASP’s Forgot Password Cheat Sheet recommends consistent responses for existing and nonexistent accounts, rate limiting, and securely generated reset tokens that expire and work only once.

If the details match an account, we’ll send instructions through an available recovery method.

The frontend message is only one part of that protection. Different response timing or API behavior can still reveal account existence.

After verifying a recovery factor, resist granting immediate access to every account feature.

A useful architecture gives the consumer a short-lived recovery session with narrowly defined permissions. For example, it might permit reviewing security settings while preventing purchases, data exports, or changes to payment information.

This type documents intent; it does not enforce security. The server must validate the session, its expiry, its association with the account, and the required permission on every request.

Check resource ownership separately. Permission to request an order review must never allow a consumer to submit another customer’s order identifier.

Do not treat an attacker’s recently changed email address as trustworthy recovery evidence. Review recovery options against previously established evidence and the account’s required level of assurance.

A new password is insufficient if an attacker still holds a valid session.

OWASP’s compromise-recovery guidance calls for reviewing recovery contacts and authentication methods, removing unauthorized changes, and invalidating existing sessions and outstanding recovery credentials after successful recovery.

For a consumer application, translate that into a visible checklist:

Use a durable workflow so a partial failure cannot silently appear as completion. Credential replacement and session revocation may span different systems; record pending work, retry failed operations safely, and show the consumer an accurate status.

JWT-based applications need particular care. Changing a password does not automatically invalidate an independently verifiable token. Choose an enforceable revocation strategy, such as checking a server-side session record or account security version on protected requests.

The OWASP Session Management Cheat Sheet provides background on session lifecycle controls.

Recovery screens are often used on a phone, in a hurry, by someone who is uncertain about what happened.

Use short steps with clear outcomes: “Review recent changes,” “Secure your sign-in,” and “Check your orders.” Explain why information is requested before asking for it.

Use native form controls with visible labels. Associate field instructions and errors with their inputs, keep keyboard navigation predictable, and move focus deliberately when a step changes. SitePoint’s HTML Form Elements guide is useful background for building these controls.

A simple reporting form might begin with:

Provide an alternative when the consumer cannot access their usual inbox. That alternative needs its own verification process; support staff should not bypass security simply because a caller sounds convincing.

Once the immediate account problem is contained, a consumer may need help addressing misuse of personal information elsewhere.

Keep this guidance separate from the application’s own recovery controls. A retailer can investigate its orders and secure its accounts. It cannot directly correct records held by unrelated organizations.

For U.S. consumers, link to the FTC’s IdentityTheft.gov as a government resource. Offer country-appropriate resources when serving other regions.

Consumers considering paid assistance can also review an identity recovery service to understand the support available for broader restoration tasks. They should check eligibility, exclusions, and service terms before relying on it.

External assistance should remain optional. Restoring access to a customer’s account should not depend on purchasing another product.

Consumers need an understandable record of what happened. Offer a case summary with relevant timestamps, reported changes, affected orders, completed actions, and unresolved questions.

Separate customer-facing information from internal investigation details. A consumer may need to know that an address changed; they do not need raw authentication tokens or internal fraud-detection rules.

Treat recovery data as sensitive. Avoid recording passwords, recovery secrets, or full payment details in logs. Restrict staff access to case information and define retention periods appropriate to its purpose.

If evidence uploads are necessary, provide a secured upload route with access controls and a deletion policy. Do not ask customers to send sensitive documents to an ordinary support inbox by default.

A successful redirect is a weak definition of recovery.

After recovery, help consumers strengthen their sign-in. SitePoint’s Developer Guide to Implementing Passkeys offers a starting point for exploring passkey authentication.

A stronger sign-in method still needs a carefully designed fallback. Evaluate the recovery path whenever you change authentication.

For a shopper, recovery succeeds when unauthorized access stops, legitimate access returns, and the next steps are clear.

Developers can support that outcome by treating recovery as a complete product journey: limited permissions, enforceable revocation, accessible interfaces, accurate case status, and clear boundaries around external assistance.

The most useful question in a design review is simple: if this were your account, could you regain control and tell what still needs attention?

#10847. Top 5 3D Animation Software in 2026

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Choosing the best 3D animation software depends on your experience, budget, and creative goals. Some tools offer complete 3D modeling and animation workflows, while others specialize in professional character animation, visual effects, or AI-powered video creation. This guide compares five options and notes how each fits into web and game projects, so designers and developers can see which one suits their pipeline.

Blender is one of the strongest free options for anyone looking for comprehensive 3D animation software. This open-source application combines modeling, sculpting, rigging, animation, rendering, simulation, and compositing in one package.

Blender provides a broad production workflow without requiring users to pay for a standard commercial license. Its active community, learning resources, and add-on ecosystem make it accessible to independent creators. It is suitable for everything from simple animated scenes to complex short films and game assets.

For web and game developers: Blender ships with a glTF 2.0 importer and exporter, and glTF is the format libraries such as three.js load for 3D on the web. See the Blender manual’s glTF page for export options.

Its extensive interface and feature set can feel overwhelming to new users. Learning advanced rigging, lighting, materials, and rendering takes time, and some established studios may have specialized pipelines built around other applications.

Beginners, freelancers, indie game developers, students, and professionals who need a powerful 3D animation tool without a software subscription.

Autodesk Maya is a professional 3D application widely associated with character animation, rigging, film production, and game development. It offers detailed control over character movement and complex animation workflows, making it a strong choice for artists working in established production environments.

Maya provides sophisticated tools for animating characters and managing complex scenes. Its established role in many professional workflows makes it a valuable skill for artists pursuing careers in film, television, and game development. It also offers detailed control over animation timing, character motion, and rig behavior.

Maya generally requires a paid subscription, which can be expensive for hobbyists and independent creators. Its advanced interface also takes time to learn, particularly for users who have never worked with professional 3D software.

Professional animators, film and television artists, game studios, and users who need advanced character animation and rigging capabilities.

Cinema 4D is a 3D modeling, animation, and rendering application known for its motion graphics workflow. It is widely used for commercial visuals, broadcast graphics, product animations, and creative work that combines 3D elements with graphic design.

Cinema 4D is a strong option for designers who want to create polished motion graphics and branded animation. Its MoGraph workflow can make repetitive or complex movements easier to manage, and its integration with common design workflows is helpful for agencies and creative teams.

For web and product designers: Cinema 4D includes a glTF exporter, which helps when a designer needs to hand a 3D model to a developer for the web. Maxon documents the options on its glTF export settings page.

Cinema 4D is a commercial product, so ongoing licensing costs may be a concern. Although it can handle a wide range of 3D work, users focused on highly specialized character animation or procedural visual effects may prefer tools designed around those workflows.

Motion designers, advertising agencies, broadcast artists, product visualization specialists, and creators who regularly produce animated graphics.

Houdini, developed by SideFX, is a 3D software package known for procedural workflows and advanced visual effects. Rather than relying entirely on manually created steps, artists can build systems that generate and modify complex scenes, simulations, and effects.

Houdini offers exceptional flexibility for technical artists and visual-effects specialists. Procedural workflows make it possible to create reusable systems and adjust complex effects efficiently. Its capabilities are particularly valuable when a project needs detailed simulations or large numbers of interconnected elements.

For game developers: SideFX offers Houdini Engine plugins for Unity and Unreal Engine, which let procedural Houdini assets be used inside those engines. SideFX explains the plugins in its Houdini Engine FAQ.

Houdini has a steep learning curve, especially for beginners who are unfamiliar with node-based workflows. Building effective procedural systems requires patience and technical understanding. While free learning options are available, some commercial uses and advanced workflows require an appropriate paid license.

Visual-effects artists, technical directors, simulation specialists, and experienced 3D creators working on demanding film, television, or game projects.

MagicLight.AI is different from the four tools above. It is an AI-powered video creation platform rather than a traditional 3D application, so it produces finished animated videos, not editable 3D scenes, rigs, or game assets. It is included here for readers who want animated storytelling without building a 3D pipeline. It supports AI-generated characters, scene creation, storyboarding, and voice features.

MagicLight.AI can reduce the time and technical effort needed to create animated videos. It is particularly useful for content creators, educators, marketers, and storytellers who want to focus on the script and final message rather than mastering a complex 3D production pipeline.

MagicLight.AI is not a replacement for dedicated 3D software when you need precise control over mesh topology, character rigs, keyframes, physics, or custom 3D scenes. Creators who need editable 3D assets for games or studio production will need a conventional 3D application such as the ones above.

Content creators, educators, and marketers who want to produce animated videos efficiently without learning a traditional 3D animation workflow.

The right software is the one that supports your actual production needs. Before choosing a tool, consider the following factors.

If you want to model objects, build character rigs, and control animation precisely, start with a dedicated 3D application such as Blender or Maya. If your goal is to create animated storytelling videos from scripts or ideas with less manual production work, an AI video tool such as MagicLight.AI may be a more convenient option.

Blender is an excellent starting point if you need a complete 3D toolset without a standard license fee. Houdini also has a free Apprentice edition with restrictions. Maya and Cinema 4D are commercial applications, so compare their current plans against your expected usage before committing.

Different applications suit different workflows. Blender is versatile, Maya excels at character animation, Cinema 4D is well suited to motion graphics, and Houdini is especially powerful for procedural effects. MagicLight.AI is better suited to AI-assisted video production than to detailed, manually controlled 3D scene creation.

Beginners may find Blender’s extensive learning resources helpful, although its broad feature set takes time to master. Maya and Houdini demand more specialized skills for advanced work, while Cinema 4D is often considered by motion designers seeking a more direct design-oriented workflow. AI video tools can reduce the amount of technical setup required, but still require creative direction and review.

Think about where the finished animation will be used. A web project may need a lightweight glTF model. A game project may require exportable 3D assets, rig compatibility, and a game-engine workflow. A commercial motion graphic may depend on rendering and compositing integration. A social video or animated explainer may prioritize turnaround time, narration, subtitles, and scene continuity.

The best 3D animation software in 2026 depends on your budget, experience, and intended output. Blender is the strongest starting point for many users who want a comprehensive free 3D workflow. Autodesk Maya is a compelling option for professional character animation, Cinema 4D suits motion graphics and commercial visuals, and Houdini stands out for procedural effects and complex simulations. MagicLight.AI offers a different approach: it helps creators generate animated videos with AI rather than manually building a traditional 3D production pipeline.

Blender is one of the best free options because it includes modeling, rigging, animation, rendering, and other production tools in a single open-source application. It is suitable for beginners as well as experienced artists.

Yes. Free software can support professional-quality work when used effectively. Blender, for example, provides a comprehensive toolset for animation and rendering. The final result depends on the artist’s skills, project requirements, hardware, and production workflow.

Blender is a strong choice for beginners who want to learn traditional 3D modeling and animation without paying for a standard software license. Those who mainly want to create animated videos from stories or scripts may prefer exploring an AI video creation platform such as MagicLight.AI.

Blender and Cinema 4D can both export glTF, a format widely used for 3D on the web. Blender is free, so it is the easiest place to start.

#10841. How to Test Location-Aware APIs with FastAPI and PostGIS

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Catch coordinate-order bugs, distance mistakes, boundary cases, and bad spatial assumptions before they reach production.

A location endpoint can return HTTP 200 and still be wrong by kilometers. That is the uncomfortable part of testing geospatial APIs: ordinary API checks tell you whether the request worked, not whether the geography is correct.

Spatial bugs can look harmless in code review. Latitude and longitude get reversed. A radius value is treated as degrees instead of meters. A query works near one city but behaves differently elsewhere because the geometry column uses the wrong type. The JSON is valid, the tests are green, and the map is still wrong.

In this tutorial, you will build a small FastAPI endpoint backed by PostGIS and then test the spatial behavior directly. The examples use deterministic fixtures positioned at known distances, so the tests verify geography rather than only response structure.

The API exposes one endpoint: GET /places/nearby. A client sends longitude, latitude, and a radius in meters. The service returns nearby places as GeoJSON features, ordered by distance.

The useful part is not the endpoint itself. The useful part is the test strategy. We will check coordinate validation, exact distance boundaries, GeoJSON coordinate order, and the database query that turns a radius into a spatial filter.

Use a geography column when the API is answering human-scale distance questions in meters. PostGIS can then calculate on the ellipsoid instead of treating longitude and latitude as flat Cartesian coordinates.

The endpoint below validates longitude, latitude, and radius with Pydantic, then sends a parameterized query to PostgreSQL. The SQL uses ST_DWithin to filter candidates and ST_Distance to return the measured distance.

The API accepts lon and lat as separate named parameters. That removes ambiguity at the HTTP boundary. GeoJSON still uses [longitude, latitude] when the response is created.

Hard-coding two real businesses and hoping their distance is approximately right makes a fragile test. A better approach is to create test points at exact distances from a fixed origin.

PostGIS can generate those fixtures with ST_Project. In the test database, create one point 500 meters away, one 950 meters away, and one 1,500 meters away. Now a 1,000-meter query has an unambiguous expected result: the first two belong in the response and the third does not.

This is the first test that actually proves something spatial. It is not checking that the endpoint returns a list. It is checking that the 1-kilometer contract means what the API says it means.

GeoJSON coordinates are longitude first, latitude second. Many developers naturally think latitude first because that is how coordinates are commonly spoken. A validation layer can catch the most obvious reversal before the database ever sees it.

Longitude must be between -180 and 180. Latitude must be between -90 and 90. That will not catch every reversal, because both numbers may sometimes fall inside both ranges, but it catches a large class of failures and gives clients a useful error instead of a misleading map.

A response can be syntactically valid JSON and still violate the GeoJSON contract. Assert the geometry type and the coordinate order. For a known query point, longitude should appear in element zero and latitude in element one.

Spatial boundaries deserve their own tests. A place at 999 meters should be included in a 1,000-meter search. A place at 1,001 meters should not. A place exactly at the boundary should behave consistently with the predicate you chose.

ST_DWithin includes features whose distance is less than or equal to the supplied threshold, so document that behavior and test it explicitly.

Unit tests are useful for request validation, but mocking the repository layer removes the most important part of this API: PostGIS itself. Keep a small integration-test database and run at least a few tests against the real extension.

That catches mistakes that mocks cannot: geography versus geometry behavior, SRID mismatches, spatial indexes, units, and changes to SQL functions. A location API is one of the cases where a cheap database integration test buys a lot of confidence.

Correctness is only half the story. A radius endpoint that scans every feature will work in development and become painful at production scale. Add a GiST index and inspect the query plan.

The exact plan depends on your data and PostgreSQL version, but you should see the spatial index participating in candidate filtering. Treat an accidental sequential scan on a large production table as a regression worth investigating.

For a real project, I would split the tests into two groups. Fast validation tests run on every commit. PostGIS integration tests run in CI with a temporary PostgreSQL/PostGIS service.

The CI gate should cover invalid coordinates, zero or negative radius values, known-distance fixtures, boundary behavior, GeoJSON output, and at least one query-plan or performance sanity check for larger datasets.

This is more work than asserting status_code == 200, but it tests the thing users actually care about: whether the API's answer is geographically true.

Location APIs fail in ways ordinary web tests are not designed to notice. The response can have the right schema, the database call can succeed, and every field can look reasonable while the location itself is wrong.

The fix is not a giant testing framework. Start with a few spatial invariants you can prove: known distances, valid coordinate ranges, correct GeoJSON order, deterministic boundary behavior, and real PostGIS integration tests. Those checks turn geography from an assumption into something your CI pipeline can verify.

#10837. 7 Checks Developers Should Make So AI Search Can Read Their Site

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Search results now include AI-written answers that cite pages, and people increasingly ask chat tools for recommendations instead of scrolling a list of links. For developers, this does not mean learning a new discipline. It means making sure the basics are in place so that crawlers and answer engines can reach, read and quote your pages.

Most problems are plain engineering mistakes: a blocked crawler, a page that renders nothing without JavaScript, or markup that disagrees with the visible text. This article covers seven checks, including one script you can run against your own robots.txt.

AI features in search do not need a special file or a special format. Google's documentation on AI features and your website states that a page needs to be indexed and eligible to appear in Search with a snippet, and that there are no additional technical requirements. It also says there is no need for new AI-specific files or special schema.org markup.

So start with the fundamentals. Check for accidental noindex tags after a deploy, confirm important pages return a 200 status, and make sure staging settings never reach production.

Answer engines read text. If your key information lives in images, canvas elements or content that only appears after a client-side script runs, a crawler may never see it. View the page source and the rendered HTML, and compare them. Product names, prices, definitions and steps should be present as real text.

Structured data helps systems understand a page, but only if it agrees with what visitors see. SitePoint's guide to JSON-LD for developers shows how to build markup that search and AI systems can parse. Generate it from the same data that renders the page, and validate it after every template change. Markup that contradicts the visible text is worse than no markup.

Your robots.txt decides which crawlers may fetch your pages. Some sites block AI crawlers deliberately, and others block them by accident through a broad rule. SitePoint's technical guide to working with a marketing agency lists robots.txt decisions about which crawlers may access the site among the checks developers should own.

This small function reads a robots.txt string and reports which common AI-related crawlers are blocked from the whole site:

Run it against your live robots.txt. If a crawler appears in the list and you did not intend that, fix the rule. Note that blocking a crawler is a legitimate choice. The goal is that it is a decision and not an accident.

Developers can check access and markup, but judging how a brand shows up in AI answers often needs someone who tracks that area full time. An AI search consultant such as Austin Heaton works on answer engine optimization.

Traffic from AI features in Google Search is included in the Search Console Performance report under the Web search type. Set up that report, tag your own campaigns with consistent UTM parameters, and watch referral traffic from chat tools in your analytics. You cannot control the answers, but you can see whether clicks arrive.

Most visibility problems appear after a deployment: a template change drops the structured data, a new rule blocks a crawler, or a redesign hides text behind a script. Add a small post-deploy test for the checks above. A failing robots.txt check or a missing JSON-LD block should stop the release before a crawler finds the problem.

Do I need an llms.txt file or special markup for AI search? Google's documentation says no new AI-specific files or special structured data are needed for its AI features. Standard indexing and crawling requirements apply.

Should I block AI crawlers? That depends on your goals. Blocking is a legitimate choice, but make it deliberately and review it regularly.

Where do I see AI search traffic? For Google, AI feature traffic is counted in the Search Console Performance report under the Web search type. Other tools show up as referrals in your analytics.

Making a site readable to AI search is mostly ordinary engineering: keep pages indexable, put key content in text, keep structured data consistent, decide crawler access on purpose, measure what you can and retest after every release. The robots.txt check above is small, but it catches one of the most common accidental blocks before it costs you visibility.

#10832. Stop Overpaying for App Builders: How to Build Premium Websites and Web Apps with AI

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Building a professional website or web application no longer requires a large development budget. AI coding agents have changed how developers approach design, development, and deployment. Yet many founders and freelancers still assume they need to pay for a dedicated AI app builder before they can create something polished.

Visual app builders such as Lovable and Bolt can help users turn ideas into working prototypes quickly. Their convenience is valuable, especially for beginners. However, subscriptions, usage limits, and additional services can increase the cost of building and maintaining a project.

There is another approach: use AI coding agents with a development stack you control.

I discovered this firsthand when I moved beyond WordPress development and built a CRM from scratch.

Instead of relying on a dedicated app builder, I used Claude, Next.js, Firebase Firestore, Tailwind CSS, Git, and Vercel.

The experience changed how I think about the cost of building software.

App builders reduce the technical barriers to software development. You describe what you want, and the platform generates interfaces, application logic, and sometimes database integrations.

The problem is not the tools themselves. It is assuming that a paid app builder is the only practical way to build a professional product.

Consider what happens when a project grows. You may need more iterations, additional integrations, more control over the generated code, or a different deployment setup. Depending on the platform and plan, these requirements can introduce additional costs or constraints.

There is also a difference between creating a prototype and building a maintainable application. Generating a dashboard is relatively straightforward. Ensuring that its permissions, database operations, error handling, and security work correctly requires additional engineering.

Paying for an app builder does not automatically solve these problems.

Before subscribing to another platform, ask a more useful question: Am I paying for a capability I genuinely need, or am I paying for a workflow I could manage directly?

I have designed more than 100 websites and helped clients implement over 12 CRM and ERP systems. My experience gave me a strong understanding of website design, business workflows, and the practical problems software needs to solve.

However, configuring existing platforms is different from developing an application from scratch.

When I decided to build a CRM, I wanted to learn the entire process, from writing code to managing data and deploying the application. I also wanted to avoid spending money on a dedicated app-builder subscription when I could use AI coding tools with a more flexible development environment.

I chose Claude as my AI coding agent and used a stack built around Next.js, Firebase Firestore, Tailwind CSS, Git, and Vercel.

This approach helped me move from requirements to implementation without depending on a single visual app-building platform. I also saved more than $1,000 in potential app-builder costs and related expenses compared with the approach I had considered.

That figure reflects my own project and spending decisions. It is not a guarantee that every developer will save the same amount.

More importantly, I gained practical experience with API integrations, application security, performance, and user experience. Those lessons became part of my technical portfolio.

You do not need to reproduce my exact stack for every project. The important idea is to choose tools that work together and give you an appropriate level of control.

An AI coding agent can help generate components, implement features, explain unfamiliar code, and investigate errors.

I used Claude to accelerate implementation and work through development challenges. Instead of asking it to build the entire application in one prompt, I worked through smaller tasks, reviewed the output, and refined the results.

The developer still needs to understand the requirements and verify the generated code. AI can accelerate development, but it cannot guarantee correctness.

Next.js provided the framework for building the web application. It supports structured development, routing, and different approaches to rendering and application logic.

For developers who want more control than a visual builder provides, working directly with a framework also makes it easier to understand how the application is organized.

Firestore provided the database layer for my project. Its document-based data model and integration with the Firebase ecosystem made it a practical choice for the application.

However, using a managed database does not eliminate the need to design data structures carefully, configure access rules, and evaluate query performance as usage grows.

A premium-looking interface depends on more than colors and typography. Spacing, consistency, responsiveness, accessibility, and clear user flows all influence the result.

Tailwind CSS helped me build and refine the interface using utility classes, making iterative design changes easier to manage.

Git allowed me to track changes, review revisions, and recover from problematic updates. Vercel provided the deployment environment and a convenient workflow for connecting releases to the codebase.

Together, these tools supported a development process that was not tied to a dedicated app-builder subscription.

A low-cost stack is only useful if the development process is disciplined.

Start by defining the application's purpose, primary users, and essential features. Then create a simple design system covering typography, spacing, colors, buttons, forms, and navigation.

Build one feature at a time. Ask the AI agent to explain its implementation, review important changes, and test each feature before expanding the application.

For a website, pay attention to responsive layouts, accessibility, loading speed, and search engine requirements. For a web application, also test authentication, permissions, database operations, and error handling.

Finally, deploy early enough to test the application in a realistic environment. Keep the code in version control and make sure you understand how to update, maintain, and recover the application.

These steps matter more than choosing whichever AI tool is currently receiving the most attention.

No. App builders can be excellent choices when speed, simplicity, and minimal setup matter most.

A founder validating an idea may benefit from launching a prototype quickly. A nontechnical user may prefer a managed platform over maintaining a codebase. A team may also find that a paid subscription costs less than the time required to manage its own development environment.

The right decision depends on the project.

If you need extensive customization, want direct control over your code, or expect to develop the application further, an AI coding agent combined with a conventional development stack may offer greater flexibility.

Compare the total cost of ownership rather than the subscription price alone. Include development time, hosting, database usage, API costs, security, maintenance, and the effort required to change platforms later.

AI has made software development more accessible, but accessibility does not mean every project needs another subscription.

My experience building a CRM taught me that a coding agent, a well-chosen technology stack, and a structured development process can be a practical alternative to a dedicated app builder.

The goal should not be to avoid spending money at all costs. It should be to spend where the investment creates genuine value.

Before paying for another tool, explore what you can accomplish with AI-assisted coding and tools you control. You may discover that the biggest limitation is not your budget, but your willingness to learn a different way of building.

Build with the tools that fit your project—not simply the tools that make the first demo easiest.
