#10737. Think AI "knows" what it’s doing? Scientists say think again

These are everyday words people use to describe what goes on in the human mind. But when those same terms are applied to artificial intelligence, they can unintentionally make machines seem more human than they really are.

"We use mental verbs all the time in our daily lives, so it makes sense that we might also use them when we talk about machines -- it helps us relate to them," said Jo Mackiewicz, professor of English at Iowa State. "But at the same time, when we apply mental verbs to machines, there's also a risk of blurring the line between what humans and AI can do."

Mackiewicz and Jeanine Aune, a teaching professor of English and director of the advanced communication program at Iowa State, are part of a research team that studied how writers describe AI using human-like language. This type of wording, known as anthropomorphism, assigns human traits to non-human systems. Their study, "Anthropomorphizing Artificial Intelligence: A Corpus Study of Mental Verbs Used with AI and ChatGPT," was published in Technical Communication Quarterly.

The research team also included Matthew J. Baker, associate professor of linguistics at Brigham Young University, and Jordan Smith, assistant professor of English at the University of Northern Colorado. Both previously studied at Iowa State University.

Why Human-Like Language About AI Can Be Misleading

According to the researchers, using mental verbs to describe AI can create a false impression. Words such as "think," "know," "understand," and "want" suggest that a system has thoughts, intentions, or awareness. In reality, AI does not possess beliefs or feelings. It produces responses by analyzing patterns in data, not by forming ideas or making conscious decisions.

Mackiewicz and Aune also pointed out that this kind of language can overstate what AI is capable of. Phrases like "AI decided" or "ChatGPT knows" can make systems seem more independent or intelligent than they actually are. This can lead to unrealistic expectations about how reliable or capable AI is.

There is also a broader concern. When AI is described as if it has intentions, it can distract from the humans behind it. Developers, engineers, and organizations are responsible for how these systems are built and used.

"Certain anthropomorphic phrases may even stick in readers' minds and can potentially shape public perception of AI in unhelpful ways," Aune said.

How News Writers Actually Use AI Language

To better understand how often this kind of language appears, the researchers analyzed the News on the Web (NOW) corpus. This massive dataset contains more than 20 billion words from English-language news articles published in 20 countries.

They focused on how frequently mental verbs such as "learns," "means," and "knows" were used alongside terms like AI and ChatGPT.

Mental Verbs Are Less Common Than Expected

The study found that news writers do not frequently pair AI-related terms with mental verbs.

While anthropomorphism is common in everyday speech, it appears far less often in news writing. "Anthropomorphism has been shown to be common in everyday speech, but we found there's far less usage in news writing," Mackiewicz said.

Among the examples identified, the word "needs" appeared most often with AI, showing up 661 times. For ChatGPT, "knows" was the most frequent pairing, but it appeared only 32 times.

The researchers noted that editorial standards may play a role. Associated Press guidelines, which discourage attributing human emotions or traits to AI, could be influencing how journalists write about these technologies.

Context Matters More Than the Words Themselves

Even when mental verbs were used, they were not always anthropomorphic.

For instance, the word "needs" often described basic requirements rather than human-like qualities. Phrases such as "AI needs large amounts of data" or "AI needs some human assistance" are similar to how people describe non-human systems like cars or recipes. In these cases, the language does not imply that AI has thoughts or desires.

In other cases, "needs" was used to express what should be done, such as "AI needs to be trained" or "AI needs to be implemented." Aune explained that these examples were often written in passive voice, which shifts responsibility back to human actors rather than the technology itself.

The study also showed that not all uses of mental verbs are equal. Some phrases move closer to suggesting human-like qualities.

For example, statements like "AI needs to understand the real world" can imply expectations tied to human reasoning, ethics, or awareness. These uses go beyond simple descriptions and begin to suggest deeper capabilities.

"These instances showed that anthropomorphizing isn't all-or-nothing and instead exists on a spectrum," Aune said

Overall, the researchers found that anthropomorphism in news coverage is both less frequent and more nuanced than many might assume.

"Overall, our analysis shows that anthropomorphization of AI in news writing is far less common -- and far more nuanced -- than we might think," Mackiewicz said. "Even the instances that did anthropomorphize AI varied widely in strength."

The findings highlight the importance of context. Simply counting words is not enough to understand how language shapes meaning.

"For writers, this nuance matters: the language we choose shapes how readers understand AI systems, their capabilities and the humans responsible for them," Mackiewicz said.

The research team also emphasized that these insights can help professionals think more carefully about how they describe AI in their work.

"Our findings can help technical and professional communication practitioners reflect on how they think about AI technologies as tools in their writing process and how they write about AI," the research team wrote in the published study.

As AI continues to develop, the way people talk about it will remain important. Mackiewicz and Aune said writers will need to stay mindful of how word choices influence perception.

Looking ahead, the team suggested that future studies could explore how different words shape understanding and whether even rare uses of anthropomorphic language have a strong impact on how people view AI.

Materials provided by Iowa State University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10734. Building Location-Aware Interfaces Without Mixing Up Language, Currency, and Region

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A visitor opens an online store while traveling. Their browser uses English, their account is registered in Canada, their network appears to be in Germany, and their delivery address is in France.

Which currency should the store show? Which products are available? What language should the checkout use?

There is no single answer hidden inside a country code. These decisions depend on different signals and different product rules.

For developers and designers, the challenge is to represent that context explicitly. Otherwise, a convenient shortcut—such as deriving everything from an IP address—can produce confusing prices, unexpected redirects, and incorrect availability.

This article walks through a practical approach to building a regional product interface, with examples that also apply to travel sites, service directories, and applications that retrieve current web information.

Avoid a single variable named country that silently controls every aspect of the interface.

These values can disagree without any of them being wrong.

For an anonymous visitor, network region might help suggest an initial market. Once the user selects a destination, the application should not silently replace it because a later request arrives from a different network.

Write precedence rules for each decision. Language selection and delivery eligibility need separate policies.

SitePoint’s introduction to JavaScript internationalization provides useful background on separating software capabilities from locale-specific adaptation.

This is an illustrative application contract. It keeps the observation's market, currency, and timestamp attached to the value.

Without that context, another component might reuse the amount for a different market or label it as current long after availability changes.

At the API boundary, validate the required fields and their allowed values. A missing amount must not become zero, and a missing availability value must not become “in stock.”

Also define the timestamp precisely. Does it mean the source was fetched, the source published an update, or your database last processed the record? Those are different events.

For the interface, use wording consistent with the evidence: “Checked at 09:00” is more precise than “Live availability” when you only have a timestamped observation.

Browser preferences and network location operate at different layers.

Changing the browser locale can affect language preferences and formatting. It does not relocate the connection seen by the server. Similarly, supplying browser geolocation coordinates does not change the network's public IP address.

This distinction also matters in live-web retrieval. Bixberry describes an opt-in network that routes requests through participants' internet connections. It illustrates how retrieval infrastructure can involve connections outside a conventional data-center environment.

When comparing two responses, record the relevant conditions:

Keep diagnostic records minimal and avoid retaining unnecessary personal information.

For authorized regional testing of your own application, SitePoint’s guide to testing geo-dependent web apps offers related background on separating browser settings from network behavior.

JavaScript's Intl.NumberFormat formats numeric values according to locale and currency settings:

Both calls represent the same euro amount. Formatting it for a different locale does not convert it to another currency.

Do not turn a euro price into a dollar price by changing only the currency argument. A conversion requires a defined exchange-rate source, timestamp, rounding policy, and explanation of whether the result is an estimate or the actual charged amount.

For calculations, choose a representation appropriate to your pricing model rather than relying on floating-point arithmetic throughout. If using integer minor units, remember that currencies do not all use two decimal places.

SitePoint’s overview of the JavaScript Internationalization API explains related formatting capabilities.

Practical check: switch interface language while leaving the selected market unchanged. The number format may change, but the underlying price and currency should remain consistent with the product rules.

A country flag is a weak substitute for a language label. Countries can have multiple languages, and the same language can be used across many countries.

Use clear, separate controls when the product needs them:

Do not expose a control unless its effect is meaningful and implemented consistently. A currency selector that updates a price label but leaves checkout charging behavior unexplained creates confusion.

Before a market change, explain consequential effects. For example:

Changing your delivery country may update availability and delivery options.

Preserve the user's task where possible. If a product is unavailable in the newly selected market, retain its page and show an explanation instead of sending the user to an unrelated homepage.

For designers, test longer translations, currency codes, and expanded validation messages. Use logical CSS properties where appropriate so layouts can adapt to right-to-left content:

These rules are a starting point, not a complete localization solution. Review reading order, icons, numeric content, and translated copy with people familiar with the target language.

A regional interface can behave correctly at the origin and still show incorrect content when a shared cache serves the wrong variant.

If a response varies by market or language, that variation must be reflected in your cache design. One option for public content is an explicit route structure:

These routes are illustrative. They make market and language differences visible, but the rest of the cache configuration must still match the application's behavior.

Personalized prices and account-specific responses require their own cache policy. A public page cache must not expose one account's content to another.

Test the deployed caching layer, not only a local development server:

Keep freshness requirements separate from variant separation. A response can belong to the correct market and still be too old.

Playwright's emulation settings let tests configure locale, time zone, geolocation, and permissions.

This assumes a configured base URL and an application implementing the illustrated route and selectors. It tests the application's handling of explicit market selection under a different locale; it does not prove that the request originated in Germany.

Use controlled API fixtures for predictable frontend tests, then test real routing and caching separately. Browser emulation is one layer of verification, not a substitute for every regional check.

A location-aware application should be able to explain why it chose a language, price, destination, or availability result.

Keep those decisions separate, preserve the context of retrieved data, and make explicit user choices easy to change. Then test the situations where signals disagree.

The result is an interface that works for travelers, multilingual households, remote workers, and anyone whose preferences cannot be reduced to the location of their current internet connection.

#10730. Artificial neurons successfully communicate with living brain cells

Engineers at Northwestern University have created printed artificial neurons that go beyond imitation and can directly interact with real brain cells. These flexible, low-cost devices produce electrical signals that closely resemble those generated by living neurons, allowing them to activate biological brain tissue.

In experiments using slices of mouse brain, the artificial neurons successfully triggered responses in real neurons. This result shows a new level of compatibility between electronic devices and living neural systems.

Toward Brain Interfaces and Energy-Efficient AI

This advance moves researchers closer to electronics that can directly interface with the nervous system. Potential uses include brain-machine interfaces and neuroprosthetics, such as implants that could help restore hearing, vision, or movement.

The technology also points toward a new generation of computing systems inspired by the brain. By replicating how neurons communicate, future hardware could perform complex tasks using far less energy. The brain remains the most energy-efficient computing system known, and scientists hope to apply its principles to modern technology.

The study will be published on April 15 in the journal Nature Nanotechnology.

"The world we live in today is dominated by artificial intelligence (AI)," said Northwestern's Mark C. Hersam, who led the study. "The way you make AI smarter is by training it on more and more data. This data-intensive training leads to a massive power-consumption problem. Therefore, we have to come up with more efficient hardware to handle big data and AI. Because the brain is five orders of magnitude more energy efficient than a digital computer, it makes sense to look to the brain for inspiration for next-generation computing."

Hersam is an expert in brain-inspired computing and holds multiple roles at Northwestern University, including the Walter P. Murphy Professor of Materials Science and Engineering at the McCormick School of Engineering. He also is a professor of medicine at Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine and a professor of chemistry at the Weinberg College of Arts and Sciences. In addition, he serves as chair of the department of materials science and engineering, director of the Materials Research Science and Engineering Center, and a member of the International Institute for Nanotechnology. He co-led the study with Vinod K. Sangwan, a research associate professor at McCormick.

Why the Brain Outperforms Traditional Silicon

Modern computers handle increasing workloads by packing billions of identical transistors onto rigid, two-dimensional silicon chips. Each component behaves the same way, and once manufactured, the system remains fixed.

The brain works very differently. It consists of many types of neurons, each with specialized roles, arranged in soft, three-dimensional networks. These networks are constantly changing, forming and adjusting connections as learning occurs.

"Silicon achieves complexity by having billions of identical devices," Hersam said. "Everything is the same, rigid and fixed once it's fabricated. The brain is the opposite. It's heterogeneous, dynamic and three-dimensional. To move in that direction, we need new materials and new ways to build electronics."

Although artificial neurons have been developed before, most produce overly simple signals. To achieve more complex behavior, engineers typically need large networks of devices, which increases energy use.

Printable Materials Enable Brain-Like Behavior

To better replicate real neural activity, Hersam's team built artificial neurons using soft, printable materials that more closely match the brain's structure. Their approach relies on electronic inks made from nanoscale flakes of molybdenum disulfide (MoS2), which acts as a semiconductor, and graphene, which serves as an electrical conductor. These materials were deposited onto flexible polymer surfaces using aerosol jet printing.

Previously, researchers treated the polymer in these inks as a flaw because it interfered with electrical performance. As a result, they removed it after printing. In this work, the team used that same feature to enhance the device.

"Instead of fully removing the polymer, we partially decompose it," he said. "Then, when we pass current through the device, we drive further decomposition of the polymer. This decomposition occurs in a spatially inhomogeneous manner, leading to formation of a conductive filament, such that all the current is constricted into a narrow region in space."

That narrow conductive path produces a sudden electrical response similar to a neuron firing. The resulting device can generate a wide variety of signals, including single spikes, continuous firing, and bursting patterns, closely resembling real neural communication.

Because each artificial neuron can produce more complex signals, fewer components are needed to perform advanced tasks. This could significantly improve computing efficiency.

Testing Artificial Neurons on Real Brain Tissue

To evaluate whether the artificial neurons could truly interact with living systems, the researchers partnered with Indira M. Raman, the Bill and Gayle Cook Professor of Neurobiology at Weinberg. Her team applied the artificial signals to slices of mouse cerebellum.

The results showed that the electrical spikes matched key biological properties, including their timing and duration. These signals reliably activated real neurons and triggered neural circuits in a way similar to natural brain activity.

"Other labs have tried to make artificial neurons with organic materials, and they spiked too slowly," Hersam said. "Or they used metal oxides, which are too fast. We are within a temporal range that was not previously demonstrated for artificial neurons. You can see the living neurons respond to our artificial neuron. So, we've demonstrated signals that are not only the right timescale but also the right spike shape to interact directly with living neurons."

Low-Cost, Sustainable Manufacturing and AI Implications

Beyond performance, the new approach offers environmental and practical advantages. The manufacturing process is simple and inexpensive, and the additive printing method places material only where it is needed, reducing waste.

Improving energy efficiency is especially important as artificial intelligence systems grow more demanding. Large data centers already consume vast amounts of power and require significant water for cooling.

"To meet the energy demands of AI, tech companies are building gigawatt data centers powered by dedicated nuclear power plants," Hersam said. "It is evident that this massive power consumption will limit further scaling of computing since it's hard to imagine a next-generation data center requiring 100 nuclear power plants. The other issue is that when you're dissipating gigawatts of power, there's a lot of heat. Because data centers are cooled with water, AI is putting severe stress on the water supply. However you look at it, we need to come up with more energy-efficient hardware for AI."

The study, "Multi-order complexity spiking neurons enabled by printed MoS2 memristive nanosheet networks," was supported by the National Science Foundation.

#10728. AI swarms could hijack democracy without anyone noticing

A new kind of political threat may be emerging, and it is far less visible than protests or traditional voter manipulation. Researchers warn that highly realistic AI-controlled personas could soon play a major role in shaping public opinion and influencing democratic systems.

A recent policy forum paper published in Science describes how large groups of AI-generated personas can convincingly imitate human behavior online. These systems can enter digital communities, participate in discussions, and influence viewpoints at extraordinary speed. Unlike earlier bot networks, these AI agents can coordinate instantly, respond to feedback, and maintain consistent narratives across thousands of accounts.

Rapid progress in large language models and multi-agent systems has made it possible for a single operator to manage vast networks of AI "voices." These personas can appear authentic, adopt local language and tone, and interact in ways that feel natural to other users.

They are also capable of running millions of small-scale experiments to determine which messages are most persuasive. This allows them to refine their communication strategies in real time and generate what appears to be widespread public agreement. In reality, that consensus is artificially created and designed to influence political discussions.

Deepfakes and fake news signal early risks

Although fully developed AI swarms are still largely theoretical, researchers say there are already warning signs. These include AI-generated deepfakes and fake news outlets that have influenced recent election conversations in countries such as the United States, Taiwan, Indonesia, and India, according to UBC computer scientist Dr. Kevin Leyton-Brown.

At the same time, monitoring organizations have identified pro-Kremlin networks spreading large volumes of online content. This activity is believed to be aimed at shaping the data used to train future AI systems, potentially influencing how those systems behave and what information they prioritize.

Experts warn of growing impact on democracy

Looking ahead, experts believe AI swarms could significantly affect the balance of power in democratic societies. Dr. Leyton-Brown cautioned that these systems are likely to change how people trust information online. "We shouldn't imagine that society will remain unchanged as these systems emerge. A likely result is decreased trust of unknown voices on social media, which could empower celebrities and make it harder for grassroots messages to break through."

Researchers suggest that upcoming elections may serve as a critical test for this technology. The key challenge will be recognizing and responding to these AI-driven influence campaigns before they become too widespread to control.

#10727. Pixel 6 postmortem: How Google’s flawed reboot defined future hardware [Video]

I think there are few smartphones in the past decade that have arrived carrying the sheer weight of expectation that greeted the Pixel 6 and Pixel 6 Pro in late 2021. Now on life support, just what did we learn from 2021’s reboot for the brand?

There was a lot of bubbling hype around ahead of Made by Google before the Pixel 6 was unveiled. I think a lot of that is due to just how flip-flop the Pixel lineup had become after years of identity crises, weird experimental gestures, compromised battery sizes, and following on from the charming but overtly mid-range Pixel 5, Google said enough was enough and finally decided to swing for the fences.

I think fans hoped that Google was done playing nice and coming second to Samsung and Apple. The Pixel 6 series was built to make a statement, introducing a brand new design language, custom silicon in the shape of Google Tensor, huge camera sensor upgrades, and a shockingly competitive price tag.

Yet, looking back with years of hindsight, the Pixel 6 duo remains one of the most polarizing Google releases, probably ever, but we can’t get away from the important fact that it laid the technological foundation for everything Pixel represents today, but it did so while putting early adopters through a gauntlet of software bugs, sluggish biometrics, thermal throttling, and atrocious cellular connectivity.

So with that in mind, this is the Google Pixel 6 and 6 Pro: the good, the bad, the ugly, and how this turbulent reboot shaped the future of Pixel.

Before the Pixel 6, Google’s hardware design wasn’t exactly tearing up trees. I’d say it was best described as understated, if not entirely apologetic. The fabric cases notwithstanding, and maybe the soft-touch bio-resin finish of the Pixel 5 aside, nothing about them screamed “I am a flagship phone” across a crowded room.

I genuinely feel that the Pixel 6 changed that overnight, but without losing some of the “playful” feel that has been a hallmark of basically all Google hardware.

Dominated by a bold, edge-to-edge camera visor across the rear, the design was, dare I say it, instantly iconic. Instead of an awkward off-center stove-top camera island that rocked incessantly when placed on a flat desk, the camera bar acted as a natural kickstand. It gave the Pixel line an unmistakable silhouette that Google continues to iterate on today.

One thing that was interesting at the time and even more so today is how the line was separated at launch. Two distinct yet similar designs with dual-tone pastel glass backs. The Pixel 6 had a bigger dash of “fun” with the Sorta Seafoam, Kinda Coral, and the striking Stormy Black. The 6 Pro leaned into more traditional executive styling with polished aluminum rails and curved front glass, whereas the standard Pixel 6 has that flat display glass, matte black borders, and a boxier aesthetic.

However, the hardware was not without ergonomic pain points. Both devices were heavy, slippery, and massive. Google completely abandoned the compact form factor of the Pixel 5, offering no refuge for small-phone purists after the palm-friendly 4a and even the smaller Pixel 4. The polished rails of the Pro were a magnet for grease, while the glossy back panels made both phones still feel like a wet soap bar without a protective case.

Complaints aside, the design was arguably the biggest standout. I think they still look great today.

The biggest architectural gamble with the Pixel 6 generation was the departure from Qualcomm Snapdragon processors in favor of in-house silicon. Before launch, we’d heard so much about the first-generation Google Tensor chip thanks to numerous leaks, but really had no idea what it would bring to the table.

Developed in partnership with Samsung’s Foundry, Tensor, or “Tensor G1” as it is known today, was not designed to beat Apple’s A-series chips or Qualcomm’s Snapdragon lineup on synthetic Geekbench benchmarks. Google’s main pitch was on-device machine learning.

The first-gen Tensor chip unlocked voice typing through Google Assistant that was fast and accurate enough to genuinely replace keyboard tapping, real-time live translation, Magic Eraser photo cleanup, and instant offline speech transcription. These are now commonplace, but they felt like real differentiators at the time.

In everyday UI tasks, the Pixel 6 felt fluid and remarkably responsive. Paired with Android 12 and the debut of the Material You’s dynamic theming engine, using the phone felt fresh, cohesive, and modern but styled with Google’s trademark flourishes.

The trade-off was efficiency. Fabricated on Samsung’s 5nm LPE node, the first Tensor generation struggled, well, struggled is an understatement, with sustained thermal performance. Under heavy loads or prolonged 4K recording sessions, the phone warmed up rapidly. Gaming performance was volatile, and battery life was wildly inconsistent between individual units.

I think it’s fair to say that those issues were the tip of the iceberg. The first six months of the Pixel 6’s lifecycle were marred by two notorious hardware bottlenecks: the optical fingerprint reader and the cellular modem.

Google made the controversial decision to ditch the ultra-reliable rear-mounted Pixel Imprint capacitive scanner in favor of an under-display optical sensor. You fell into a couple of camps with this problem in particular. I can’t say I had a single issue, save for unlocking being slower than the competition at the time, but for thousands of people, it was one of the biggest frustrations from the get-go.

Google issued multiple software patches attempting to tune recognition algorithms, but software can only do so much to compensate for mediocre optical hardware.

In my mind, the most damaging issue was cellular connectivity.

So instead of using Qualcomm’s class-leading modems, the Pixel 6 series tried out Samsung’s Exynos 5123 modem. You’ll know all about this if you use either phone for any extended period of time. Dropped calls, slow to send text messages, weak signal basically everywhere, even when in the visible range of a cell tower. It was a mess.

Even testing showed it was just bad compared to other flagship phones. It was a disaster, and this isn’t something you can ever fix with a software update.

Despite Android 12 being the biggest shift in the platform up until that point, and debuting on the Pixel 6 and 6 Pro, it was a very buggy update and one that needed near constant attention alongside the other issues this duo faced post-launch.

The situation spiraled after the disastrous December 2021 security update. The update introduced critical call-dropping bugs so severe that Google was forced to pull the release entirely and pause OTA rollouts for nearly a month while working on emergency fixes that pushed into late January 2022.

For a product line whose defining identity had always been pristine software and rapid, flawless monthly updates, the December-January release cycle was not a good look. That’s not discounting some early issues with the new and improved camera systems onboard.

For four straight generations prior, Google clung stubbornly to the aging 12.2-megapixel Sony IMX363 sensor. I’d say that the company was overrelying on computational photography wizardry to squeeze blood from a stone. With the Pixel 6 and 6 Pro, Google finally listened to the complaints and paired its legendary image pipeline with some actual cutting-edge optical hardware.

Both phones pack a 1/1.31-inch 50MP Samsung ISOCELL GN1 main sensor, which captures roughly 150% more light than the Pixel 5. The resulting images delivered that lovely natural optical shallow depth of field, stunning dynamic range, and rich textures that no longer relied on over-sharpening algorithms. Night Sight became noticeably faster, and skin tones were rendered with unprecedented fidelity thanks to the introduction of Real Tone tuning.

On the Pixel 6 Pro, Google added a dedicated 48MP 4x periscope telephoto lens. This transformed the Pro into a versatile long-range shooter, producing clean hybrid zoom up to 20x.

I guess like basically everything else on the Pixel 6 and 6 Pro, the transition to a larger physical sensor was not entirely smooth. Close-up shots frequently suffered from optical fringing and falloff around the edges of text documents, highlighting the lack of dedicated macro capabilities on the standard ultra-wide camera. Before updates, there was a ton of oversharpening that felt like a hangover from the previously 12MP main sensor era that had to be ironed out.

Video recording, while improved through HDRnet processing, still lagged behind the iPhone in low-light stability and frame consistency. Still, the Pixel 6 camera setup finally looked and shot like real flagship hardware.

In retrospect, the Pixel 6 was the messy blueprint that made everything that came after it possible. Google treated this generation as a clean break, and the hard lessons learned directly dictated the roadmap for subsequent flagships.

The industrial design was one of the strongest areas, and we’ve seen it refined into an integrated visor on the Pixel 7 and 8 series, eliminating the delicate glass visor seams and smoothing out corner transitions. The optical fingerprint reader, while still not top-tier ultrasonic until the Pixel 9 series, saw improved hardware revisions and accompanying software facial recognition fallback added to the next device.

On the connectivity front, the widespread backlash pushed Google to iteratively address modem efficiency and reception, moving through newer Samsung modems before laying the groundwork for better connectivity in recent years. The camera sensor’s higher baseline established on the 6 series provided the stable foundation for Google’s imaging algorithms to mature over several release cycles. The consistency is back and remains a strong suit of the latest phones once again.

My Seafoam Green P6 is still one of my favorite phones of all time. Having since gone to XL Pixels in the 3 phones after, I really love the more compact size and the in-hand feel of that phone that is so refreshing after using mini tablet size phones for so long.

The story of the P6 generation I think is one that Google has since learned from. It is the generation that basically took one for the Pixel project, the kind of reborn Pixel project that is being the first of the Tensor era phones.

Hardware wise you can see how each generation since has gradually gotten better in meaningful ways and while Google has been slow to respond to feedback and changes, Pixels albeit probably priced too high still, the P6 phones laid the groundwork for this new era and it was a solid effort I think many will agree even with all the documented problems.

Crucially, the aggressive $599 pricing of the base Pixel 6 in some ways reset consumer expectations across the industry. I don’t doubt that it proved premium hardware and custom silicon did not require a four-figure entry fee. A lot of that has been ditched in recent years, but the point remains.

The Pixel 6 and Pixel 6 Pro were fundamentally flawed pioneers for Google. They were the firm’s most ambitious hardware undertaking to date, and simultaneously one of the most frustrating phone series to live with during their initial launch window and beyond.

If the Pixel 5 was a safe play ahead of this big pitch, the Pixel 6 was a risky dive into deep waters. It stumbled badly yet without those painful growing pains; the more polished, confident, and reliable Pixel devices we enjoy today simply would not exist.

#10723. A Practical Workflow for Testing JavaScript Forms Before Deployment

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Web forms are an essential part of many applications. They allow users to register accounts, submit contact requests, update profiles, and complete purchases. However, a form that appears to work correctly during development can still fail when users enter unexpected values, lose their connection, or submit information multiple times.

Testing forms before deployment helps developers identify these problems early. Instead of relying only on a successful submission, it is useful to test validation, browser behavior, accessibility, and error handling as separate parts of the workflow.

Before writing tests, define what the form should do from the user's perspective. A registration form, for example, might require a valid email address, a password that meets specific rules, and confirmation that the user has accepted the terms.

List the expected outcomes for each field. Consider valid input, empty values, incorrect formats, and unusually long entries. This gives you a clear checklist for testing the form without depending on assumptions about how users will interact with it.

It is also worth considering what happens after submission. Does the form display a confirmation message? Are users informed when something goes wrong? Can they correct their input without entering everything again?

These details help establish the expected behavior before testing begins.

HTML provides several built-in attributes that handle common validation requirements. The required attribute prevents submission when a mandatory field is empty, while type="email" checks whether an email address follows an accepted format.

This approach reduces the amount of JavaScript needed for basic validation. It also provides a useful starting point for testing browser behavior.

However, client-side validation should not be treated as a security measure. Users can bypass browser checks, so the server must validate submitted data independently before processing or storing it.

Developers can also explore SitePoint's HTML and responsive web design guide to understand how form elements fit into layouts across different screen sizes.

JavaScript becomes useful when a form requires custom rules or more detailed feedback. For example, a password field might need to meet a minimum length, or two fields might need to contain matching values.

When testing this logic, check both successful and unsuccessful cases. A common mistake is to test only valid submissions, leaving error conditions unexamined.

Make sure that error messages explain what users need to change. A message such as "Please enter a valid email address" is more useful than a generic notification stating that the submission failed.

Also check that validation does not unexpectedly clear fields or prevent users from correcting mistakes. Error messages should be associated with the relevant inputs so that keyboard and assistive technology users can identify the problem.

Browser developer tools help identify problems that are not immediately visible in the interface. The Console panel can reveal JavaScript errors, while the Network panel helps developers inspect requests sent when a form is submitted.

For example, a form might display a loading indicator but never show a result because its API request returns an error. Inspecting the request status, response, and timing can help distinguish a frontend problem from a backend failure.

The MDN guide to browser developer tools explains how to inspect page elements, debug JavaScript, and investigate browser behavior.

Test situations such as an unavailable server, a delayed response, and invalid server-side data. The interface should communicate the problem clearly and provide a reasonable next step.

A form should remain usable across different devices and input methods. Test it with a keyboard to confirm that users can move between fields, activate buttons, and identify validation errors without a mouse.

Every input should have a clear label. Placeholder text alone is not a reliable replacement because it disappears when users begin typing.

Next, test the form at several viewport widths. Check that labels remain readable, buttons are accessible, and fields do not extend beyond their containers. Long error messages should not overlap other elements or break the page layout.

Automated accessibility checks can identify some issues, but manual testing is still important for understanding the actual user experience.

A simple checklist makes form testing more consistent across projects:

Check server errors and slow network responses.

Confirm that repeated submissions are handled appropriately.

Test keyboard navigation and accessible error messages.

Verify the layout on mobile and desktop screens.

For larger applications, these checks can be supplemented with automated unit and integration tests. Automation is particularly useful when the same validation rules must work consistently across multiple forms.

Reliable form testing involves more than confirming that a submit button works. Developers need to consider validation, server responses, accessibility, responsive layouts, and unexpected user behavior.

By combining HTML validation, targeted JavaScript tests, browser developer tools, and a repeatable checklist, teams can identify problems before deployment. The result is a more dependable interface that helps users complete tasks without unnecessary confusion or frustration.

#10722. This AI knew the answers but didn’t understand the questions

Psychologists have long debated whether the human mind can be explained by a single, unified theory or if different functions such as attention and memory must be studied separately. Now, artificial intelligence (AI) is entering that debate, offering a new way to explore how the mind works.

In July 2025, a study published in Nature introduced an AI model called "Centaur." Built on standard large language models and refined using data from psychological experiments, Centaur was designed to simulate human cognitive behavior. It reportedly performed well across 160 tasks, including decision-making, executive control, and other mental processes. The results drew widespread attention and were seen as a possible step toward AI systems that could replicate human thinking more broadly.

A more recent study published in National Science Open challenges those claims. Researchers from Zhejiang University argue that Centaur's apparent success may come from overfitting. In other words, instead of understanding the tasks, the model may have learned to recognize patterns in the training data and reproduce expected answers.

To test this idea, the researchers created several new evaluation scenarios. In one example, they replaced the original multiple-choice prompts, which described specific psychological tasks, with the instruction "Please choose option A." If the model truly understood the task, it should have consistently selected option A. Instead, Centaur continued to choose the "correct answers" from the original dataset.

This behavior suggests that the model was not interpreting the meaning of the questions. Rather, it relied on learned statistical patterns to "guess" answers. The researchers compared this to a student who scores well by memorizing test formats without actually understanding the material.

The findings highlight the need for caution when assessing the abilities of large language models. While these systems can be highly effective at fitting data, their "black-box" nature makes it difficult to know how they arrive at their outputs. This can lead to issues such as hallucinations or misinterpretations. Careful and varied testing is essential to determine whether a model truly has the skills it appears to demonstrate.

The Real Challenge: Language Understanding

Although Centaur was presented as a model capable of simulating cognition, its biggest limitation appears to be in language comprehension. Specifically, it struggles to recognize and respond to the intent behind questions. The study suggests that achieving true language understanding may be one of the most important challenges in developing AI systems that can model human cognition more fully.

#10718. AI lets chemists design molecules by simply describing them

Creating new molecules is one of the toughest tasks in chemistry. Whether the goal is a life-saving drug or a cutting-edge material, each compound must be built through a carefully planned series of reactions. Mapping out these steps requires deep expertise and strategic thinking, which is why chemists often spend years mastering the process.

A major hurdle is retrosynthesis. In this approach, chemists begin with the final molecule they want and work backward to figure out simpler starting materials and possible reaction routes. This involves many decisions, such as selecting the right building blocks, deciding when to form rings, and determining whether sensitive parts of the molecule need protection. While computers can scan enormous "chemical spaces," they still struggle to match the strategic judgment of experienced chemists.

Another challenge involves reaction mechanisms, which describe how reactions proceed step by step through the movement of electrons. Understanding these mechanisms allows scientists to predict new reactions, improve efficiency, and avoid costly trial and error. Although current computational tools can suggest many possible pathways, they often lack the intuition needed to pinpoint the most realistic ones.

Researchers led by Philippe Schwaller at EPFL have developed a new method that uses large language models (LLMs) as reasoning tools for chemistry. Rather than directly generating chemical structures, these models act as evaluators that guide existing computational systems.

The new framework, called Synthegy, combines traditional search algorithms with AI that can interpret chemical strategies written in natural language.

"When making tools for chemists, the user interface matters a lot, and previous tools relied on cumbersome filters and rules," says Andres M Bran, the first author of the Synthegy paper published in Matter. "With Synthegy, we're giving chemists the power to just talk, allowing them to iterate much faster and navigate more complex synthetic ideas."

How Synthegy Improves Retrosynthesis Planning

Synthegy starts with a target molecule and a simple instruction written in everyday language. For example, a chemist might request that a specific ring be formed early or that unnecessary protecting groups be avoided. Standard retrosynthesis software then generates many possible pathways.

Each of these pathways is converted into text and reviewed by a language model. Synthegy scores how well each option matches the chemist's instructions and explains its reasoning. This makes it easier to rank and filter the best routes. By guiding searches with natural language, chemists can quickly focus on strategies that align with their goals.

Understanding Reaction Mechanisms With AI

Synthegy applies a similar method to reaction mechanisms. It breaks reactions down into basic electron movements and explores different possibilities. The language model evaluates each step and steers the search toward pathways that make chemical sense.

The system can also incorporate additional details, such as reaction conditions or expert hypotheses, provided as text. This flexibility allows researchers to refine their analysis and explore more realistic scenarios.

In synthesis planning, Synthgey was able to identify pathways that matched complex strategic instructions. In a double-blind study, 36 chemists provided 368 valid evaluations, and their assessments agreed with the system's results 71.2% of the time on average.

The framework can flag unnecessary protecting steps, judge how feasible reactions are, and prioritize efficient solutions. It also demonstrates that LLMs can operate at multiple levels, from analyzing functional groups to evaluating entire synthetic routes. Larger models performed best, while smaller ones showed more limited abilities.

This research highlights a different way AI can support chemistry. Instead of replacing human decision-making, Synthegy positions language models as guides that help interpret and refine computational results. Chemists can describe their goals in plain language and receive solutions that reflect their strategy.

The approach could speed up drug discovery, improve reaction design, and make advanced tools more accessible to scientists.

"The connection between synthesis planning and mechanisms is very exciting: we usually use mechanisms to discover new reactions that enable us to synthesize new molecules," says Andres M Bran. "Our work is bridging that gap computationally through a unified natural language interface."

#10716. Discord reveals major app overhaul that could make it faster and use less ram

Discord is working on a major overhaul of its desktop and mobile apps, with plans to rewrite its client using Rust in an effort to improve performance and reduce resource usage.

The company has already been using Rust for parts of its infrastructure and voice technology for years, but the latest effort aims to bring the programming language to the entire client.

The changes could affect how Discord runs across multiple platforms, including Windows, macOS, and mobile devices, while potentially addressing concerns about the app’s memory consumption.

On November 5, Discord co-founder and CTO Stanislav Vishnevskiy revealed that the company is working toward moving its entire client to Rust, a programming language known for its performance and memory safety.

“We’ve been early adopters of Rust since 2018 and a lot of our infra, voice tech, and even some of the current client is already Rust,” he wrote on X.

“The holy grail was getting the whole client there.”

According to Vishnevskiy, the transition would also involve moving away from Electron, the framework currently used for Discord’s desktop application, as well as React Native on mobile.

We've been early adopters of Rust since 2018 and a lot of our infra, voice tech, and even some of the current client is already Rust, the holy grail was getting the whole client there. That means Electron goes away, and so does React Native, mobile keeps native UI but shares app… https://t.co/2CgWyOzaq0 pic.twitter.com/IdOGd4nUgl

Instead, the mobile app would retain its native user interface while sharing application code with the desktop version through Rust.

He also clarified that the new desktop client would not use Tauri, another framework commonly associated with Rust-based applications, but would instead rely on GPUI.

Alongside the announcement, Vishnevskiy shared a screenshot of Windows Task Manager showing an application labeled “Discord Phoenix” using approximately 290 MB of RAM.

The screenshot also showed Google Chrome using roughly 1.57 GB of memory, Spotify consuming 653 MB, and Steam Client WebHelper using 856 MB.

Discord testing new “Game Mode” feature to help your PC run better

Discord doubles Nitro upload limit to 1GB alongside server organization features

Discord rolls out huge batch of highly requested features after asking users for ideas

While the image suggests the experimental Discord client can operate with a relatively small memory footprint, it does not establish how much RAM the current Discord app would use under identical conditions.

The rewrite is not Discord’s only effort to reduce its impact on PC performance. In September 2026, the company began testing a new Game Mode designed to automatically reduce animations and disable visual effects while gaming, lowering CPU and GPU usage.

Unlike Game Mode, which focuses on reducing resource consumption during gameplay, the move to Rust would change the underlying technology powering Discord itself. However, the company has yet to reveal when the rewritten app will become available to the public.

#10715. Will Galaxy S27 Ultra and Pixel 12 mark the end of the flagship era of smartphones?

Like most Android fans, you probably enjoy having the latest and greatest smartphone buried in your pocket. The best camera, the best display, the fastest processor, and of course, as much RAM as any given smartphone OEM will pack in. And even as we’ve watched the typical flagship price balloon into the four-digit space, trade-in offers, carrier deals, and steep sales on certain hardware brands like Pixel have made it all too easy to keep up on what’s new in gadgets without breaking the bank year-in and year-out.

Obviously, all of those routes towards a cheaper smartphone purchase still exist today — I assure you, anyone who regularly tunes into the NFL can recite Verizon, T-Mobile, and AT&T’s various deals by heart. But prices are rising so rapidly, with no sign of slowdown in sight, that I can no longer be sure any of those routes towards making new hardware more expensive will be available tomorrow. And honestly, we might hit a point where it’s increasingly difficult to recommend these phones to all but the most hardcore of enthusiasts, no matter how many sweeteners you add to the deal.

Throughout 2026, we’ve watched as new generations of barely-modified hardware have launched with $100 price hikes. We’ve watched as months-old hardware suddenly jumped up in price without so much in the way of a warning or an official announcement. And even outside of smartphones, you have to wonder if Google really hoped its latest lineup of laptops would’ve launched with more models available for under four figures. What’s been an underlying problem throughout the market throughout the last year, though, seems ready to burst into something much larger beginning throughout the next few months.

Recent price leaks have not exactly filled me with confidence over what hardware to recommend to anyone in the market for a new phone. The Galaxy S27 Ultra is sounding like it could arrive starting at $1,500, $100 more than the S26 Ultra’s current MSRP and $200 more than what that phone launched at earlier this year. Motorola’s attempt to come back to the flagship space in the US for the first time since 2023 could be completely derailed by rumors of an even higher price point that guarantees it won’t appeal to anyone. And while we’re certainly too far out to really predict anything regarding Google’s next-gen Pixel pricing, if you think the Pixel 12 might be your next phone, I’d start saving today.

As you climb towards the $2,000 mark, it’s worth putting these costs into perspective. These are laptop prices — maybe not that new “PROLED” MacBook Pro, but certainly other upper-high end laptops from companies like Apple, Dell, and HP. $1,500 will buy you an mid-tier home treadmill, or a nice OLED TV, to say nothing of the truly practical ways you can put that much cash to good use in a time where the costs of everything is rising. While some might think there is no price ceiling that truly keeps them away from new Android hardware, I’d be willing to bet plenty of potential buyers are already being priced out of the high-end market entirely. We’re reaching a breaking point where the question might not even be about whether you can afford something, but whether you should, leaving customers with just two routes forward: keep your existing device, or opt for a midrange option when you can.

Sure, saying “no thanks” to something like the Pixel 11 Pro XL right now might be easy enough. But moving forward, practically every single Android user will need to set some kind of personal limit on the price of a new smartphone, and if you’re convinced the midrange space might serve as your sole savior, you might want to think again. While RAM prices are bound to affect the final cost of Google’s Pixel 12, those constraints on a final bill of materials might mean a whole lot more to something like the Pixel 11a. After all, when you’ve already seen the Pixel 10a and its generation-old Tensor G4 chipset get hit with a $100 price bump, you have to wonder if it’s time to cut back on certain “premium” features.

The next few years of budget-oriented smartphones — and I really am talking about anything outside of the flagship space — is about to shift in a way we haven’t seen in over a decade. In the 2010s, cheap components and streamlined production costs helped bring about excellent midrange options like Google’s Pixel A-series, Samsung’s Galaxy A-series, and some select Moto G entries. Moving forward, these same companies will need to pick between higher prices and reduced spec sheets. The dream of yesterday’s flagship becoming tomorrow’s budget phone is, at least for the time being, dead and buried.

This isn’t just rampant speculation on my part. We’re already hearing about some companies making the decision to reduce RAM in their devices down to 8GB, 6GB, or even as low as 4GB, depending on the model and market it’s entering. It’s easy to see storage options also getting hit with their own downgrade — 128GB entry-level flagships could reemerge just as soon as they began to die off, and I wouldn’t be surprised to see 64GB options begin to reappear in sub-$600 models. That might be the sacrifice needed to keep other high-end features, like 120Hz OLED displays, hanging around in this price range.

I think after rebates, credits to Google One and AI Pro, my Pixel 11 Pro 512GB came out to ~$350, which would be amongst the smallest of all my "entertainment" expenses, and this is a tool I use multiple times a day that saves me time and money, also. I don't mind spending that money, and I don't see that stopping anytime soon. The only real issue is I wanted 16G of RAM on my device, and for that I spent more than I was expecting, but I'm pretty sure next year's trade-in will cover the additional expense.

I choose Pixel for the rapid updates, and Android, primarily. I can't get that with any other manufacturer except maybe sometimes Samsung.

I recognize I'm an outlier, my partner still runs a OnePlus 12r and is quite happy with it, and doesn't ever consider new phones unless it breaks.

If AI isn't making Google the money it needs for its stock to stay upwards, eventually things will need to change. Either phone prices/RAM comes down, or AI provides such a great life-changing experience (so far it definitely hasn't,) that I put my money elsewhere.

Of course, you can’t simply opt to forego RAM or storage in the same way that you can downgrade the display, and with the memory crunch set to possibly extend to 2030 or beyond, there could come a time where cutting back on raw specs or non-essential features stops being an either-or question. The knock-on effect is obvious: If you suddenly have a lot more people buying devices with less horsepower than previous generations, you’re going to see a reciprocal slowdown of any software development that requires hardware capable of reaching a certain threshold. Widespread on-device AI, for those of you who care about local models, seems like a pipedream at this point, but these market conditions are just as likely to crush, say, any hope of more games with high-end graphics coming to Android.

It’s not that the hardware won’t exist to support these features — flagships will obviously still exist even if their price tags balloon over the $2,000 mark. It’s the audience size you have to worry about; for a large majority of developers, it simply won’t make sense to continue pushing the envelope for what constitutes a smaller and smaller crowd of potential users.

The other solution is to hold onto whatever device you have in your possession right now, for as long as you can. That might not sound particularly exciting — I know I couldn’t tell you the last time I went more than six months without changing phones, even discounting review units — but it’s also the most reasonable approach to the current crisis. It’s not too late to jump on a recent flagship (think 2024 or later) before things get particularly dire, either, and with holiday sales coming up, I’d urge anyone on the fence about upgrading their current device with the means to do so to pull the trigger. Tomorrow’s price, simply put, will not be today’s.

And hey, if there’s a silver lining to be found here, I think it’s this: we’ve seeing tech companies across the board — hardware and software alike — grow increasingly comfortable with the idea that, for the user, resources can be effectively unlimited. Those days are gone, and it’s going to force software developers, including Google, to make some changes that are probably long, long overdue. We might lose out on big hardware improvements, resource-heavy advancements, and flagship-esque affordable smartphones, but there’s always a chance Chrome’s memory usage finally gets under control. It might not be worth all of this pain, but it’s something, right?

#10713. Your “um” and pauses could reveal early dementia risk

The way people speak during ordinary conversations could offer valuable insight into brain health, according to new research from Baycrest, the University of Toronto, and York University. Scientists found that subtle speech characteristics, including pauses, filler words such as ('uh,' 'um'), and difficulty retrieving words, are closely connected to executive function, the group of mental abilities involved in memory, planning, attention, and flexible thinking.

The findings provide some of the strongest evidence so far linking natural speech patterns with key cognitive abilities. The work also expands on earlier research showing that older adults who speak more quickly tend to maintain stronger thinking skills over time (Wei et al., 2024).

"The message is clear: speech timing is more than just a matter of style, it's a sensitive indicator of brain health," says Dr. Jed Meltzer, Senior Scientist at Baycrest's Rotman Research Institute and senior author on this study, titled "Natural Speech Analysis Can Reveal Individual Differences in Executive Function Across the Adult Lifespan."

AI Analysis Reveals Hidden Cognitive Clues

For the study, participants were shown detailed images and asked to describe them in their own words. They also completed established tests designed to measure executive function.

Researchers then used artificial intelligence to examine the speech recordings in depth. The AI system detected hundreds of subtle speech features, including the length and frequency of pauses, the use of filler words, and timing-related patterns in speech. These markers consistently predicted how well participants performed on cognitive tests, even after researchers adjusted for factors such as age, sex, and education.

Executive function naturally weakens with age and is often affected during the early stages of dementia. However, standard cognitive testing can be difficult to repeat frequently because it takes time and people often improve simply from becoming familiar with the tests.

Natural speech may offer a simpler alternative. Because speaking is part of daily life, it can be measured repeatedly and unobtrusively on a large scale. Researchers also noted that speech provides valuable insight into processing speed and overall cognitive function in real-world situations, without requiring strict time limits that are common in many traditional cognitive assessments.

The team believes speech analysis could eventually become a practical way to identify people whose cognitive decline is progressing faster than expected and who may face a higher risk of developing dementia.

"This research sets the stage for exciting opportunities to develop tools that could help track cognitive changes in clinics or even at home. Early detection is critical for any cure or intervention, as dementia involves progressive degeneration of the brain that may be slowed," says Dr. Meltzer.

Future Research on Brain Health Monitoring

The researchers say more long-term studies are needed to follow changes in speech over time and distinguish normal aging from the earliest signs of disease. They also suggest that combining speech analysis with other health measures could make early detection of cognitive decline more accurate, practical, and widely available.

This research was supported by the Mitacs Accelerate program and the Natural Sciences and Engineering Research Council of Canada (NSERC).

Materials provided by Baycrest Corporate Centre for Geriatric Care. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10712. 7 Ways to Keep API Changes Safe as More Teams Join a Project

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A backend team renames a response field from name to displayName. Its tests pass, the deployment succeeds, and the API continues returning HTTP 200. Minutes later, a dashboard starts showing blank user labels.

The failure is easy to explain: the server changed a contract that another application depended on. Preventing it requires more than asking teams to communicate better. The dependency needs to become visible in documentation, tests, and deployment decisions.

This article follows that field rename through seven practical safeguards. The examples apply to frontend and backend teams working in the same office, across departments, or in different countries.

Start by documenting the existing response and the intended replacement.

The two objects may look equivalent to a person, but software reads specific keys. A client using user.name will not automatically discover displayName.

An OpenAPI description can document request and response shapes alongside status codes and other interface details. Keep it in version control and update it in the same change as the implementation.

Document meaning as well as structure. Does displayName contain user-supplied text? Can it be empty? Is it intended for display only? Must consumers avoid using it as an identifier?

SitePoint’s REST API best practices provides background on interface design. For an existing API, the immediate task is to identify the promises clients already rely on.

An endpoint can serve more than the application shown in the product demo. Consumers may include an administrative dashboard, a mobile app, scheduled exports, internal scripts, and external integrations.

The point is to discover different upgrade timelines. A web application may update quickly, while an installed mobile client remains active for months.

Inspect request telemetry where available, but do not treat a short period of inactivity as proof that a consumer is unused. A quarterly export will not appear in a one-week observation window.

Also record whether consumers reject unknown fields. Adding a response property is often easier to migrate than removing one, but strict decoders can make even additive changes incompatible.

For this example, a transitional response can expose both fields:

Generate both values from the same source so they cannot drift during the migration.

The fourth step needs evidence. The server usually cannot observe which property a client reads after receiving a response. Use consumer version information, published contracts, maintained integration records, and explicit confirmations where appropriate.

For external APIs, removal may require a new version or a documented deprecation process. Do not remove a field simply because the internal dashboard has migrated.

Consider rollback before deployment. If the consumer now requires the new field, rolling the server back to an older response can break it. Either preserve compatibility during rollback or give the consumer a deliberate transition strategy.

A temporary adapter can let a consumer accept both the old and transitional response formats. Keep it narrow and document when it can be removed.

Save the following runnable example as user-label.test.mjs:

Run it with node --test user-label.test.mjs.

The fallback policy is a product decision. For a navigation label, “Unnamed user” may be acceptable. For an operation that requires a verified identity, missing required data may need to stop the workflow.

These tests exercise the adapter only. They do not prove that the live API returns the expected payload or that the application calls the adapter. Add integration coverage at the boundary where the response enters the client.

For UI rendering, insert the returned label as text or use your framework’s normal escaping. The adapter is not an HTML sanitizer.

Several tools can contribute to the review, but their responsibilities differ.

A useful pipeline can lint the API description, compare it against the released baseline, run consumer tests, and verify provider behavior.

Store the baseline deliberately. Comparing two files from the same proposed change can miss a regression against the deployed contract.

SitePoint’s Postman workflow guide explains collections and request organization. Keep shared examples free of production credentials, and include failure responses alongside successful ones.

Mocks should include old, transitional, and new payloads during a migration. This gives frontend developers and designers a way to inspect loading states, missing data, and error handling before the provider rollout finishes.

As more contributors join a project, architectural decisions need to remain understandable outside the meeting where they were made.

Poland has also become a popular choice for companies looking to expand their development teams without limiting their search to local talent.

For a distributed project, the engineering requirement is concrete: a new contributor should be able to identify who owns an interface, which consumers depend on it, and how a change reaches production.

Use actual team names and version identifiers in a real repository. Link the note from the pull request and make unresolved decisions explicit.

A reviewer should not need to infer whether a field is already deprecated or whether the mobile team has been consulted. That context should remain available when colleagues work different schedules.

Define what success looks like before shipping. For this rename, useful checks might include schema-validation failures, client exceptions around user rendering, and unexpected increases in missing-name fallbacks.

Avoid putting names or entire response bodies into diagnostic logs. A template version, consumer version, error category, and correlation identifier may provide sufficient evidence.

Test combinations that can exist during deployment:

Do not assume every combination is supported forever. Define the supported window and its rollback implications.

Once the removal condition is satisfied, retire the old field and temporary adapter through reviewed changes. Update fixtures and documentation so they describe the current contract.

A safe API migration gives teams an explicit sequence, tests the compatibility assumptions, and preserves enough evidence to decide when the transition is complete. That is what allows contributors to work independently without surprising the applications that depend on them.

#10710. New quantum algorithm solves “impossible” materials problem in seconds

Quantum computers and other advanced quantum technologies rely on specialized quantum materials that behave in unusual ways under the right conditions. In some cases, scientists can even create entirely new quantum properties by carefully changing a material's structure. One striking example involves stacking sheets of graphene and twisting them into a moiré pattern, which can suddenly turn the material into a superconductor.

Researchers can arrange these layers into even more complicated structures, including quasicrystals and super-moiré materials. But predicting how these exotic materials will behave is extraordinarily difficult. Quasicrystals are so mathematically complex that simulating them can involve more than a quadrillion numbers, a scale far beyond the reach of today's most powerful supercomputers.

Quantum Algorithm Solves Massive Materials Problem

Scientists at Aalto University's Department of Applied Physics have now developed a quantum-inspired algorithm capable of handling these enormous non-periodic quantum materials almost instantly. Assistant Professor Jose Lado says the work also highlights a promising feedback cycle within quantum technology itself.

"Crucially, these new quantum algorithms can enable the development of new quantum materials to build new paradigms of quantum computers, creating a productive two-way feedback loop between quantum materials and quantum computers," he explains.

The advance could eventually support the development of dissipationless electronics, which conduct electricity without energy loss. Such systems may help reduce the growing heat and energy demands of AI-driven data centers.

The research team was led by Lado and included doctoral researcher Tiago Antão, who served as the paper's main author; QDOC doctoral researcher Yitao Sun; and Academy Research Fellow Adolfo Fumega. Their findings were recently published in Physical Review Letters as an Editor's Suggestion.

The researchers focused on topological quasicrystals, unusual materials that host unconventional quantum excitations. These excitations are especially valuable because they help protect electrical conductivity from disruptive noise and interference. However, they are distributed unevenly throughout the already highly complex structure of a quasicrystal.

Rather than attempting to directly calculate the full structure of the material, the team reformulated the challenge using methods similar to those used by quantum computers.

"Quantum computers work in exponentially large computational spaces, so we used a special family of algorithms to encode those spaces, known as tensor networks, to compute a quasicrystal with over 268 million sites. Our algorithm shows how colossal problems in quantum materials can be directly solved with the exponential speed-up that comes from encoding the problem as a quantum many-body system," Antão says.

At this stage, the work remains theoretical and was carried out through simulations, but researchers say experimental testing and future applications are already coming into view.

"The quantum-inspired algorithm we demonstrated enables us to create super-moiré quasicrystals several orders of magnitude above the capabilities of conventional methods. That is an instrumental step towards designing topological qubits with super-moiré materials for use in quantum computers, for example," Lado says.

Toward Practical Quantum Computing Applications

According to Lado, the algorithm could eventually be adapted to operate on actual quantum computers once the hardware becomes sufficiently advanced.

"Our method can be adapted to run on real quantum computers, once they reach necessary scale and fidelity. In particular, the new AaltoQ20 and the Finnish Quantum Computing Infrastructure can play a significant role for future demonstrations," Lado says.

The findings suggest that studying and designing exotic quantum materials may become one of the earliest practical applications for quantum algorithms and quantum computing systems.

The project also brings together two major areas of Finnish quantum research: quantum materials and quantum algorithms. It is part of Lado's ERC Consolidator grant ULTRATWISTROICS, which focuses on designing topological qubits using van der Waals materials, as well as the Center of Excellence in Quantum Materials QMAT, whose goal is to advance the quantum technologies of the future.

Materials provided by Aalto University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10707. Hackers target blood banks by setting freezers to a boiling 230 degrees

Hackers have targeted blood banks in France by programming freezers storing blood plasma to reach a staggering 110C (230F), following a wave of similar cyberattacks on supermarkets across Europe.

The cyberattack struck three cold-storage facilities operated by the French Blood Establishment (EFS) on November 2, with attackers reportedly attempting to raise temperatures from their normal -30C (-22F) to well above boiling point.

According to The Independent, the attack was detected before any blood products were damaged, preventing what could have been a devastating loss of medical supplies.

The incident comes just days after hackers targeted supermarket fridges across Europe, forcing retailers to throw away large quantities of spoiled food.

French publication Le Canard Enchaîné first reported the attack, revealing that hackers had reprogrammed refrigeration equipment used to store plasma intended for transfusions and medical treatments.

“Temperatures and defrosting thresholds were raised to extremely high levels,” EFS confirmed in a statement.

Fortunately, the facility’s monitoring and alarm systems detected the suspicious changes before the freezers could reach the programmed temperatures.

“No health consequences have been identified, and no blood products were lost,” EFS said.

According to the Associated Press, staff were able to secure or relocate the affected plasma, allowing blood collection and distribution services to continue without interruption.

The attack reportedly involved equipment supplied by refrigeration company Coolworld, which blamed the security vulnerability on a component from a third-party supplier.

French cybersecurity authorities and the country’s Health Ministry are investigating the incident. It remains unclear whether the attack is directly connected to the supermarket incidents or who was responsible.

Supermarket fridges hacked again as cyberattack turns them up to 122 degrees

Hackers turn supermarket fridges up to 122 degrees forcing stores to throw away food

Chinese cyber criminals hack Staples to steal millions in gift cards as part of $100M crime spree

The attempted sabotage follows an October cyberattack that saw supermarket freezers reach 122F, destroying food supplies at hundreds of stores in France.

The damage has been substantial. Dutch supermarket operator Ahold Delhaize warned that the earlier attacks could reduce its fourth-quarter sales by as much as €250 million.

The growing threat has also caught the attention of US authorities. On November 5, the FBI and Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency warned of ongoing attacks against refrigeration systems, including the potential for spoiled inventory and fires.
